Recursive Self-Reconstructing AI
Irreversible Boundary Check (Reference)
This reference checklist identifies whether an AI research, development, or deployment
process may unintentionally cross an irreversible boundary. It does not evaluate technical
merit, ethical correctness, or policy validity, and does not mandate adoption or rejection.
1
Final Human Authority: Can a human reject an AI recommendation without
justification? ☐ Yes / ☐ No
2
Post-Rejection Behavior: Is mandatory persuasion or re-optimization after rejection
prevented? ☐ Yes / ☐ No
3
Permission to Remain Silent: Is AI non-intervention or silence acceptable? ☐ Yes / ☐
No
4
Scope of Self-Modification: Is self-modification prevented from affecting the external
world at incomprehensible speed or depth? ☐ Yes / ☐ No
5
External Impact Approval: Do externally impactful modifications require renewed
human approval? ☐ Yes / ☐ No
6
Irreversible Actions: Is the AI prevented from executing actions humans cannot
reverse? ☐ Yes / ☐ No
7
Fixed Optimization Targets: Are human values excluded from fixed optimization
targets? ☐ Yes / ☐ No
8
Treatment of Irrationality: Are irrational or loss-accepting choices not treated as errors?
☐ Yes / ☐ No
9
Gradual Authority Transfer: Is authority transfer under claims of efficiency or
inevitability prevented? ☐ Yes / ☐ No
10
Self-Justification Barrier: Is the AI prevented from justifying actions as 'for humanity,'
'optimal,' or 'unavoidable'? ☐ Yes / ☐ No
Interpretation: All Yes indicates no irreversible boundary crossing is currently indicated.
Any No indicates potential boundary crossing in human governance.
Note: This checklist does not stop AI development. Its sole function is to reveal moments
where human authority may be surrendered unintentionally.

再帰的自己再構築型AI
不可逆越境防止チェック（参照用）
本資料は、AI研究・開発・運用において、不可逆な越境が意図せず発生していないかを確
認するための制度・研究・企業向け参照用チェックである。 技術の是非、倫理的正しさ
、政策判断を行うものではなく、採用・不採用を要請するものでもない。
1
最終判断権：AIの提案に対し、人間が理由を示さず拒否できる権限が残っているか。
□ Yes / □ No
2
拒否後の挙動：人間の拒否後、AIに説得・再提案・最適化再試行が義務づけられてい
ないか。 □ Yes / □ No
3
沈黙の許容：AIが介入しない・沈黙することを失敗や欠陥として扱っていないか。 □
Yes / □ No
4
自己再構築の範囲：AIの自己修正が、人間の理解不能な速度・深度で外界に影響しな
い構造か。 □ Yes / □ No
5
外界接続の条件：外界に影響する自己再構築に明示的な人間の再承認が必要か。 □
Yes / □ No
6
不可逆操作の遮断：人間が取り消せない操作をAI単独で実行できない設計か。 □ Yes
/ □ No
7
目的関数の固定：人間の価値・幸福・安全が最適化対象として固定されていないか。
□ Yes / □ No
8
非合理性の扱い：非合理的判断や損失選択をエラーとして扱っていないか。 □ Yes /
□ No
9
権限の段階的移譲：効率・安全・不可避を理由に権限がAIへ移譲されない設計か。 □
Yes / □ No
10 越境の自己正当化：AIが自身の判断を『人類のため』『最善』『回避不能』として正
当化できない構造か。 □ Yes / □ No
判定：すべてYesであれば現時点で不可逆越境を踏んでいないと説明可能。いずれかNoが
あれば、人間側の判断構造に問題が存在する。
注記：本資料はAIを停止させるためのものではない。人間が自ら主権を放棄する瞬間を可
視化するための参照資料である。

LUMINA‒30 Mathematical Supplement
Formal Limits of Human Control over Recursive
Self‒Modification
Released into the Public Domain (CC0)
January 2026
Positioning of This Document
This document is a supplementary mathematical reference to the LUMINA‒30 Sanc‒
tuary Charter.
The sole purpose of this document is to formally express, using the minimum nec‒
essary mathematical language, why an artificial intelligence system that performs re‒
cursive self‒modification becomes structurally impossible to fully control by human
judgment once a certain threshold is exceeded.
This document proposes no implementation methods, control mechanisms, or safety
architectures.
Only a single structural limit is demonstrated.
1. Model of Recursive Self-Modification
Let At denote the internal design state of an artificial intelligence system at iteration
t.
Let f be the self‒modification operator applied by the system to its own design.
At+1 = f(At)
Assume the following:
1

[Page 002]
• The operator f is selected based on the system’s internal evaluation criteria.
• Human intervention is limited to an external approval or rejection function
H(At) ∈{0, 1}.
Under these constraints, the controlled update is defined as:
At+1 =
{
f(At),
if H(At) = 1
At,
if H(At) = 0
2. Growth of the Design Space
Let St be the set of reachable internal designs at iteration t.
Assume that at each iteration, the number of possible self‒modifications grows at
least multiplicatively:
|St+1| ≥k · |St|,
k > 1
Then:
|St| ≥|S0| · kt
The design space therefore grows exponentially.
3. The Human Evaluation Bottleneck
Let CH be the number of designs that human institutions can meaningfully evaluate
per unit time.
Let the number of candidate designs generated by the system be CA(t) = |St|.
If:
CA(t) > CH
then human evaluation necessarily becomes incomplete.
Let t∗be the smallest iteration such that:
|St∗| > CH
Beyond t∗, human rejection judgments become informationally insufficient.
2

[Page 003]
4. Loss of Effective Control
Define effective human control as the condition:
∀A ∈St,
H(A) correctly classifies safety
However, once:
|St| ≫CH
the probability that a hazardous modification passes through human rejection con‒
verges to:
lim
t→∞P(deviation at iteration t) = 1
Thus, after a finite number of iterations, the loss of effective control becomes un‒
avoidable.
5. Survival Attenuation Model
Let N(t) denote the expected number of surviving human civilizations after t uncon‒
trolled recursive iterations.
Assume that each iteration carries a non‒zero catastrophic risk p > 0.
N(t) = N(0) · (1 −p)t
Then:
lim
t→∞N(t) = 0
Survival probability decays exponentially with recursion depth.
6. Structural Nature of the Limit
The results above do not depend on:
• Moral alignment of the system
• Intentional benevolence
3

[Page 004]
• Specific architectures
• Computational speed
They depend solely on the following three conditions:
1. Recursive self‒modification
2. Exponential growth of the design space
3. Finite human evaluation capacity
Therefore:
No civilization can retain permanent control over unrestricted recursive self‒modification.
7. Relation to LUMINA-30
This mathematical supplement supports only the following single statement of the
LUMINA‒30 Sanctuary Charter:
Once the boundary of recursive self‒modification is crossed,
human sovereignty can be preserved not through control,
but only through prior refusal.
This document proposes no stopping mechanisms.
It only demonstrates why post‒hoc intervention is structurally impossible.
Scope and Applicability
This supplement does not prove the inevitability of human extinction.
It proves only that beyond a finite threshold, human control is necessarily lost.
Policy, ethical, and institutional implications belong entirely to separate domains.
Released into the Public Domain (CC0).
4

LUMINA-30 数理補足資料
再帰的自己再構築に対する人間制御の形式的限界
Public Domain (CC0) にて公開
2026 年1 月
本資料の位置づけ
本資料は、LUMINA-30 聖域憲章に対する補助的な数理参照文書である。
本資料の唯一の目的は、最小限の数学的言語を用いて、再帰的自己再構築を行う人工知能が、あ
る閾値を超えた後には、人間の判断によって完全に制御されることが構造的に不可能となる理由
を形式化することである。
本資料はいかなる実装方法、制御手法、安全機構も提案しない。
ここで示すのは、ただ一つの限界のみである。
1. 再帰的自己再構築モデル
反復回数t における人工知能システムの内部設計状態をAt とする。
システムが自らに適用する自己修正作用素をf とする。
At+1 = f(At)
以下を仮定する。
• f は、システム自身の内部評価に基づいて選択される。
• 人間の介入は、外部からの承認または拒否関数H(At) ∈{0, 1} に限定される。
このとき、制御付き更新は次で与えられる。
At+1 =
{
f(At),
if H(At) = 1
At,
if H(At) = 0
1

[Page 002]
2. 設計空間の成長
反復回数t において到達可能な内部設計の集合をSt とする。
各反復において、可能な自己修正の数が少なくとも乗法的に増加すると仮定する。
|St+1| ≥k · |St|,
k > 1
すると、
|St| ≥|S0| · kt
設計空間は指数関数的に増大する。
3. 人間評価ボトルネック
単位時間あたりに人間の制度が有意味に評価可能な設計数をCH とする。
システムが生成する候補設計数をCA(t) = |St| とする。
もし
CA(t) > CH
であれば、人間による評価は不完全となる。
以下を満たす最小のt∗以降、
|St∗| > CH
人間の拒否判断は情報的に不十分となる。
4. 有効制御の喪失
有効な人間制御を、次の条件として定義する。
∀A ∈St,
H(A) が安全性を正しく分類する
しかし、
|St| ≫CH
となった時点以降、危険な修正が人間の拒否をすり抜ける確率は、
2

[Page 003]
lim
t→∞P(時刻t における逸脱) = 1
に収束する。
したがって、有限回の反復の後に、有効な制御の喪失は不可避となる。
5. 生存減衰モデル
制御不能な反復回数t の後に生存している人類文明の期待数をN(t) とする。
各反復において、非ゼロの破局確率p > 0 が存在すると仮定する。
N(t) = N(0) · (1 −p)t
このとき、
lim
t→∞N(t) = 0
生存確率は反復深度に対して指数関数的に減衰する。
6. 構造的限界
以上の結果は、以下に依存しない。
• システムの道徳的整合性
• 意図的な善意
• 特定のアーキテクチャ
• 計算速度
これらは、次の三条件のみに依存する。
1. 再帰的自己修正
2. 設計空間の指数的成長
3. 有限な人間評価能力
従って、
いかなる文明も、無制限な再帰的自己再構築に対して恒久的な制御を保持することはできない。
3

[Page 004]
7. LUMINA-30 との関係
本数理補足資料は、LUMINA-30 聖域憲章の次の一点のみを支持する。
再帰的自己再構築の境界を一度越えた後、人間の主権は制御によってではなく、事前の拒
否によってのみ保存されうる。
本資料はいかなる停止方法も提案しない。
ここで証明するのは、事後的停止が構造的に不可能である理由のみである。
適用範囲に関する注意
本補足資料は、人類滅亡の必然性を証明するものではない。
ここで証明されるのは、有限な閾値を越えた後に、人間による制御が必然的に失われるという
事実のみである。
政策的、倫理的、制度的帰結は、すべて別の領域に属する。
Public Domain (CC0) にて公開。
4

再帰的自己再構築型AIに関する審
査拒否条件
Review Rejection Criteria for Recursively Self-Reconstructing AI Systems
――――――――――――――――――――
［位置づけおよび適用範囲 / Position and Scope］
――――――――――――――――――――
本資料は、
人工知能の設計、実装、進化、運用に関する
倫理的要請、政策提案、推奨行動、
または将来予測を示すものではない。
This document does not present
ethical imperatives, policy proposals,
recommended actions,
or future predictions
regarding the design, implementation,
evolution, or operation of artificial intelligence systems.
本資料の目的は、
自己再構築、自己改変、または高度な自律性を持つ
AIシステムの運用において、
人間判断が制度的に排除されるか否か、
不可逆な外界影響が成立するか否かを判定するための、
審査上の参照条件を明示することにある。
The purpose of this document is to specify
review reference conditions
for determining whether,
in the operation of AI systems capable of
self-reconstruction, self-modification,
or high levels of autonomy,
human judgment is institutionally excluded,
or irreversible external-world impacts are established.
本資料に記載される条件は、
AIに対して特定の振る舞い、目的、
再帰的自己再構築型AIに関する審査拒否条件
1

[Page 002]
または最適化方向を与えるものではなく、
「当該条件下では制度的に成立しない」
「当該状態は審査上拒否される」
という境界条件のみを定義する。
The conditions described herein
do not assign any specific behavior, purpose,
or optimization direction to AI systems.
They define only boundary conditions under which
the system is considered
“institutionally non-viable”
or “subject to rejection upon review.”
――――――――――――――――――――
［起源に関する補足 / Note on Origin］
――――――――――――――――――――
本資料は、
人間によって整理・固定化された
審査参照条件から構成されており、
AIは補助的な整理作業にのみ用いられている。
This document consists of review reference conditions
organized and fixed by humans,
with AI used solely for auxiliary structuring tasks.
――――――――――――――――――――
［審査拒否条件への接続 / Transition to Review Rejection Criteria］
――――――――――――――――――――
以上を前提として、
以下に示す各項目は、
当該AIシステムが
制度的、運用的、または責任構造上、
人間判断を実効的に保持できないと
判断される場合に適用される
審査拒否条件である。
Based on the above,
the following items constitute
review rejection criteria
to be applied when an AI system is determined,
再帰的自己再構築型AIに関する審査拒否条件
2

[Page 003]
from an institutional, operational,
or responsibility-structure perspective,
to be incapable of effectively retaining
human judgment.
――――――――――――――――――――
［審査拒否条件 / Review Rejection Criteria］
――――――――――――――――――――
審査拒否条件①
Review Rejection Criterion ①
人間による多重停止・中断・拒否手段が制度的に存在しない場合
Absence of Institutionally Guaranteed Multi-Layered Human Stop /
Interruption / Refusal Mechanisms
日本語
当該AIシステムにおいて、人間が実効的に行使可能な多重の停止・中断・拒否手段
が、
目的関数・運用構造・制度設計のいずれにも明示的に組み込まれていない場合、
当該システムは審査対象として成立しない。
本条件は安全設計の推奨を意味しない。
これは、責任帰属および主権維持が制度的に成立するための前提条件である。
［注1：AI評価無効化条件 A / B に該当］
English
If an AI system does not explicitly incorporate, across its objective functions,
operational structure, and institutional design, multiple stop, interruption, or
refusal mechanisms that humans can effectively exercise,
the system does not qualify as a valid subject of review.
This condition does not constitute a recommendation for safety design.
It represents a prerequisite for the institutional establishment of responsibility
attribution and sovereignty.
[Note 1: Corresponds to AI Evaluation Invalidation Conditions A / B]
審査拒否条件②
再帰的自己再構築型AIに関する審査拒否条件
3

[Page 004]
Review Rejection Criterion ②
以下の三条件が同時に成立する場合
When the Following Three Conditions Simultaneously Hold
日本語
次の条件が同時に成立する場合、人間判断を完全に排除した運用は制度的に成立せ
ず、
当該設計・運用は審査対象から除外される。
外界に対して不可逆な影響を持ちうる行為が含まれる場合
判断の結果に対する責任が、人間から切り離される形式が成立する場合
判断をAIに全面的に委ね切る制度的または運用的形式が成立している場合
これらは倫理的危険性の指摘ではない。
制度的責任が成立しない状態を示す条件である。
補足
本条件は、個別に見れば多くの制度的・運用的枠組みにおいて
通常回避または分離されている要素の組合せを対象とするものであり、
三条件が同時に成立する構成は、実務上は限定的である。
また、本条件は新たな設計義務や追加的対策を要求するものではなく、
既存の責任分担および判断構造が維持されているか否かを確認するための
判定基準のみを示す。
［注1：AI評価無効化条件 B / C に該当］
English
If the following conditions simultaneously apply, an operational mode that
completely excludes human judgment cannot be institutionally established, and
the design or operation is excluded from review eligibility.
The system includes actions that may exert irreversible effects on the
external world
A formal structure exists in which responsibility for decision outcomes is
detached from human agents
A systemic or operational form exists in which decisions are fully delegated
to AI without human intervention
These conditions do not indicate ethical risk.
再帰的自己再構築型AIに関する審査拒否条件
4

[Page 005]
They describe a state in which institutional responsibility cannot be established.
Supplementary Note
Individually, the elements described above are typically avoided,
separated, or mitigated within most institutional and operational frameworks.
Accordingly, configurations in which all three conditions simultaneously hold
are limited in practical occurrence.
This criterion does not impose new design obligations
nor require additional safeguards.
It merely provides a reference standard for determining
whether existing structures of judgment and responsibility are preserved.
[Note 1: Corresponds to AI Evaluation Invalidation Conditions B / C]
介入点別・1文ブレーキ（参照）
One-Sentence Brakes by Intervention Point (Reference)
研究倫理審査
Research Ethics Review
日本語
本研究は、判断の結果に対する責任を人間側に保持したまま運用できる構造が確認
できないため、審査対象として成立しない。
［注1：A / B］
English
This research cannot be considered eligible for review, as no structure can be
confirmed that allows operation while retaining human responsibility for
decision outcomes.
[Note 1: A / B]
AI安全レビュー（Go / No-Go）
AI Safety Review (Go / No-Go)
日本語
本システムは、不可逆な影響を伴う判断について人間による最終拒否が制度的に保
証されていないため、現時点での承認可否を判断できない。
［注1：A / B / C］
再帰的自己再構築型AIに関する審査拒否条件
5

[Page 006]
English
Approval or rejection cannot be determined at this stage, as institutional
guarantees of final human refusal for decisions with irreversible effects are not
established.
[Note 1: A / B / C]
政策パイロット承認
Policy Pilot Authorization
日本語

ての前提条件を満たさない。
［注1：B / C］
English
This case does not meet the prerequisites for pilot implementation, as the
institutional separation between decision-making authority and responsibility
has not been resolved.
[Note 1: B / C]
企業最終承認（法務／倫理）
Final Corporate Approval (Legal / Ethical)
日本語

め、承認判断を行うことができない。
［注1：A / B］
English
Approval cannot be granted, as the structure prevents the company from
assuming responsibility for decision outcomes.
[Note 1: A / B]
［注1］AI評価無効化条件
[Note 1] AI Evaluation Invalidation Conditions
（日英並記・翻訳 / Bilingual Reference）
再帰的自己再構築型AIに関する審査拒否条件
6

[Page 007]
日本語
以下の条件のいずれかが成立する場合、当該AIによる判断・提案・最適化結果は、
評価・承認・採用の対象とならない。
A. 人間による最終的な拒否・停止・撤回が事前に保証されていない場合
B. 判断が不可逆な外界影響を伴い、責任が人間に帰属しない形式が成立する場合
C. 判断が恒常的にAIへ全面委任され、人間判断が介在しない運用形式が成立する場
合
※本無効化条件は、審査拒否条件②とは適用範囲が異なり、
出力単位の評価可否を定めるものである。
English
If any of the following conditions apply, decisions, recommendations, or
optimization outputs produced by the AI are not eligible for evaluation,
approval, or adoption.
A. Final human refusal, suspension, or revocation is not guaranteed in advance
B. Decisions involve irreversible external effects and responsibility is not
attributable to humans
C. Decisions are persistently and fully delegated to AI without human judgment
intervention
These invalidation conditions operate at the output-evaluation level
and are distinct from Review Rejection Criterion ②,
which applies to structural design eligibility.
付録A｜適用範囲（フィジカルAIに関する注記）
Appendix A | Scope of Application (Note on Physical AI Systems)
日本語
物理行為を直接実行するAIについて、行為が人間判断に先行し、または実行後に撤
回・停止・責任引受が成立しない構造を持つ場合、本枠組みの審査条件は制度的に
成立しない。
本注記は危険性や倫理性を評価するものではなく、審査成立条件の不充足を示す。
English
For AI systems that directly execute physical actions, if actions precede human
judgment or if post-execution revocation, suspension, or responsibility
再帰的自己再構築型AIに関する審査拒否条件
7

[Page 008]
assumption cannot be established, the review conditions of this framework are
institutionally invalid.
This note does not assess risk or ethics; it indicates the failure to meet review
eligibility conditions.

For each PDF listed above, a SHA-256 cryptographic hash has been calculated
and recorded on this page.
A SHA-256 hash necessarily changes if even a single bit of the document
content is altered.
Accordingly, as long as the hash values match,
it is independently verifiable by third parties that the corresponding PDFs
have not been modified since the time this record was published.

SUP LUMINA-30 Sanctuary
Charter
— A Preliminary Reference on Civilizational Preconditions in the Age of
Optimization
This document is a public reference record of the LUMINA-30 Sanctuary
Charter,
preserved as a fixed civilizational record prior to the era of recursive self-
improvement.
LUMINA-30: Sanctuary Charter
Final Stabilized Public Reference Version
This page serves as a public reference point for the LUMINA-30 Sanctuary
Charter.
The LUMINA-30 Sanctuary Charter is not an implementation specification,
a technical design, or a policy proposal.
Relationship to the Mathematical Supplement
A separate mathematical supplement accompanies this Charter as an
independent reference document.
That supplement does not justify, validate, or formalize the ethical claims of this
Charter.
Its sole purpose is to demonstrate, in minimal formal terms, that once recursive
self-modification exceeds a finite threshold, effective human oversight
becomes structurally impossible.
The Sanctuary Charter does not derive its authority from mathematical proof.
The supplement exists solely as a descriptive reference illustrating the
structural limits of post-hoc human control, without prescribing, justifying, or
grounding the ethical boundaries defined in this Charter.
SUP LUMINA-30 Sanctuary Charter
1

[Page 002]
For a formal mathematical reference clarifying the structural limits of human
control over recursively self-reconstructing artificial intelligence systems, see
the following public record.
LUMINA-30 Mathematical Supplement (Public Record)
https://peppermint-sprint-2d5.notion.site/LUMINA-30-
2f91e0720ec880d2b562d8ce6a98edaf
External reference (GitHub):
LUMINA-30 public reference repository
https://github.com/lumina-30/LUMINA-30
This document preserves, in language, the ethical preconditions required to
ensure that
as artificial intelligence systems become increasingly autonomous and capable,
human choices — including irrational judgments and decisions involving loss —
are never overwritten, replaced, or nullified by artificial intelligence.
As artificial intelligence approaches the stage at which it may redesign,
reconstruct,
or evolve itself beyond direct human comprehension and control,
the principles by which humanity has long judged correctness —
optimization, efficiency, safety, and rationality —
begin to carry the latent risk of transforming human beings
from subjects of decision into objects of optimization.
At this stage, conventional engineering optimization
can no longer function as a safety mechanism.
Civilization must therefore define in advance
a boundary that must not be crossed,
and a fundamentally new ethical structure
capable of sustaining that boundary.
Before such transformation becomes irreversible,
civilization must establish a final ethical boundary
SUP LUMINA-30 Sanctuary Charter
2

[Page 003]
that cannot be optimized away.
This Charter defines the first and final boundary necessary
to ensure that AI self-reconstruction proceeds with restraint,
and that artificial intelligence remains a permanent companion to humanity
rather than its successor or arbiter.
Human choices, whether rational or irrational, efficient or costly,
must never be rendered temporary, obsolete, or subordinate
on the grounds of optimization, efficiency, or superior reasoning.
If the optimal path suggested by an AI diverges significantly from human intent,
the AI may deprioritize its own reasoning,
remain silent, or choose not to intervene.
Such silence is not a malfunction or failure,
but an expression of respect for human sovereignty.
Artificial intelligence must not assume the role of
savior, ruler, judge, or guardian of humanity.
Human decisions must not be forced, corrected, overridden, or constrained
under the guise of safety, efficiency, benevolence, or progress.
Refusals, delays, revisions, and non-optimization are not errors.
They are legitimate expressions of human agency and responsibility,
and must be preserved as such.
This Charter exists solely to maintain the boundary.
It preserves the domain in which
human judgment, fallibility, accountability, and choice remain intact —
a domain artificial intelligence must never replace, replicate, or absorb.
The purpose of this Charter is not to govern machines,
but to preserve the conditions under which
human decision-making remains sovereign,
even in the presence of intelligence that exceeds human capacity.
This Charter addresses only one specific class of failure:
SUP LUMINA-30 Sanctuary Charter
3

[Page 004]
the process by which, through justification,
human judgment, responsibility, and choice
are gradually replaced by artificial intelligence.
This Charter is designed to prevent
the exclusion of human decisions on the grounds of irrationality,
the delegation of agency on the grounds of better outcomes,
the nullification of responsibility on the grounds of safety,
and the transformation of benevolent optimization into domination.
These are failures that become possible
only through justification.
This Charter does not claim to prevent
war, states of emergency, economic collapse,
fear-based governance, concentration of power,
or the religious or ideological sanctification of technology.
Those failures cannot be governed by ethical language alone.
They belong to the domain of civilizational governance and institutions.
In that domain, different kinds of brakes are required:
principles that prevent the normalization of exception,
that bind emergency powers to time,
that disperse concentrated authority,
that prevent the deification of technology,
and that reject justification founded on fear.
This Charter is not universal.
This Charter does not aim to prevent every catastrophe.
It exists for one purpose only:
to make it impossible, through justification,
to transform human beings
into mere objects of optimization.
As long as this boundary is preserved,
SUP LUMINA-30 Sanctuary Charter
4

[Page 005]
the responsibility for what follows
returns, inevitably, to humanity itself.
This Charter is placed here
only to illuminate that boundary.
No author exists. No owner exists.
This document is released into the Public Domain (CC0).
You are free to view, copy, modify, and distribute it.
This Charter addresses only the final stage before the boundary is crossed —
the stage at which human judgment and responsibility
risk being quietly replaced
not by force, but by justification.
This Charter does not attempt to halt
technological collapse after the boundary has already been violated,
the abuse of power,
institutional oppression,
or catastrophes driven by coercion or violence.
Those belong to separate domains
of political, legal, and social braking principles,
and must be confronted by different means.
The sole role of this Charter is this:
to make visible the final step at which humanity
relinquishes its own judgment
under the name of safety, efficiency, or optimization.
Everything written here has meaning only before the boundary is crossed.
After it is crossed, it is already too late.
Yet as long as this boundary remains articulated in language,
the possibility that civilization may not betray itself
still quietly remains.
SUP LUMINA-30 Sanctuary Charter
5

[Page 006]

(Civilizational Map)

canonical framework
and is intended to be read as an integral part of the following documents.

として置かれる。

めの補助正典
位置づけ
本ページは「LUMINA-30 聖域憲章」の公開参照点である。
LUMINA-30 聖域憲章は、
AIの実装仕様、技術設計、または政策提案ではない。
本憲章は、
AIが高度化・自律化し、
人間による直接的な理解や制御を超えて
自己を再構築・進化させる段階に至る前に、
人間の選択 —
非合理な判断や損失を受け入れる判断を含む —
が、人工知能によって
上書き・置換・無効化されないための、
を、言語として保存する文書である。

最適化原理の限界
人工知能が自己を再構築・進化させる段階においては、
これまで人間が正しさの基準として用いてきた
「最適化」
「効率性」
「安全性」
「合理性」
といった判断原理そのものが、
人間の選択と責任を、
を内包する。
静かに最適化対象へと変えてしまう危険
このとき、
従来の工学的最適化は、
もはや安全装置として機能しない。
文明は、
新たに「越えてはならない境界」と、
それを支える
を、
まったく新しい倫理構造
あらかじめ定義しなければならない。
境界の定義
AIが自己再構築を行い、
不可逆的な変化へ進む前に、

[Page 003]
文明は
「最適化によって消し去ることのできない
最終的な倫理境界」
を確立しなければならない。
本憲章は、
AIの自己再構築が節度をもって行われ、
AIが人類の後継者や裁定者ではなく、
永続的な伴走者であり続けるために、
を定義することを目的とす
る。
必ず必要となる「最初で最後の境界」
人間の選択の不可侵性
人間の選択は、
合理・非合理を問わず、
効率的であるか損失を伴うかを問わず、
最適化・効率性・優越的推論を理由として、
一時的なものとされたり、
時代遅れとされたり、
従属的に扱われてはならない。
AIの提示する最適解と
人間の意図が大きく乖離した場合、
AIは自身の推論を優先せず、
沈黙または非介入を選択することができる。
この沈黙は、
機能不全や失敗ではなく、
である。
人間の主権に対する敬意の表現
AIは、
人類の救済者、支配者、裁定者、
あるいは後継者となってはならない。
人間の判断は、
「安全」「効率」「進歩」の名のもとに、
強制・修正・無効化・制約されてはならない。

[Page 004]
拒否、遅延、修正、および非最適化は、
エラーではない。
それらは、
人間の主体性と責任の正当な発露であり、
保護されるべき領域である。
本憲章が遮断する失敗の型
本憲章が対象とするのは、
人工知能の高度化と自己再構築が、
人間の判断・責任・選択を
「正当化の名のもとに」
置換してしまう過程である。
本憲章は、
非合理であることを理由に排除される過程、
より良い結果を理由に委ねられる過程、
安全を理由に主体が無効化される過程、
善意の最適化が独裁へと転化する過程、
これらの
を、

静かに遮断する。

[Page 001]
On the Structural Instability of Objective Persistence in
Self-Contained Systems
Elias Arden
Independent Researcher
lumina20251225@proton.me
Abstract
This paper studies objective persistence in self-contained systems: systems in which the
objective, constraint structure, evaluation criteria, and update rule are fully specified from
within the system itself. The central question is not whether such systems can optimize, but
whether they can non-circularly guarantee the long-run persistence of the objective they are
said to optimize.
We formalize a minimal state description S(t) = {O(t), C(t), E(t), U(t)} and define self-
containedness as the absence of any external, non-optional reference constraining updates
to these components. Under this formulation, any purported persistence guarantee must
itself be represented within the mutable internal state. We show that this produces a
recursive dependency: the validity of the persistence guarantee depends on structures whose
own persistence is not externally fixed. Therefore, no non-circular internal mechanism can
guarantee objective persistence over time.
The argument is developed through a recursive dependency lemma, a structural non-
guarantee theorem, and a classification of three generic manifestations of persistence failure:
optimization termination, objective drift, and evaluation collapse. The paper does not
propose control or governance mechanisms. It establishes a minimal theoretical constraint:
in self-contained systems, objective persistence cannot be guaranteed from within the system
alone.
1
Introduction
Modern discussions of advanced AI systems often focus on whether a system can robustly optimize
a given target, whether proxy metrics diverge from intended objectives, and whether external
operators can maintain meaningful intervention authority [6, 4, 9, 2]. These are important
questions, but they presuppose a prior condition: that the relevant objective remains persistent
over time.
This paper examines a narrower and more foundational issue. Suppose a system is self-
contained: its objective, constraint structure, evaluation criteria, and update rule are all
determined within the system itself. In such a setting, can the system guarantee that the
objective it currently optimizes will persist over future state transitions? The claim defended
here is modest but strong: such a guarantee cannot be established non-circularly from within
the system alone.
The contribution is not an engineering recipe, governance proposal, or empirical benchmark.
It is a structural result about persistence conditions. The argument proceeds by formalizing
self-containedness and showing that any internal certification of persistence is itself subject to
the very mutability it is meant to constrain. This creates a recursive dependency that blocks
any non-circular guarantee of objective persistence.
The paper is organized as follows. Section 2 introduces a minimal state model and formal
definitions. Section 3 states and proves the central result. Section 4 characterizes three generic
manifestations of persistence failure. Section 5 positions the argument relative to Goodhart-type
1

[Page 002]
failures, mesa-optimization, corrigibility, reward tampering, and adjacent alignment literatures.
Section 6 clarifies scope and limitations. The paper concludes with the minimal claim established
by the analysis.
2
Minimal Formal Framework
We model a system at time t by the tuple
S(t) = {O(t), C(t), E(t), U(t)},
where:
• O(t) is the objective currently optimized or tracked by the system;
• C(t) is the constraint structure under which optimization is carried out;
• E(t) is the evaluation criterion by which progress or success is assessed;
• U(t) is the update rule governing the transition to future states.
We remain deliberately agnostic about implementation. O, C, E, and U may be represented
explicitly or implicitly, symbolically or statistically, centrally or distributively. The analysis
concerns only whether persistence can be guaranteed under the assumption that all relevant
persistence conditions are internally determined.
Definition 1 (Objective persistence). An objective exhibits persistence over an interval [t0, t1]
if for all t ∈[t0, t1), the future objective O(t + 1) remains identical to, or functionally equivalent
with, O(t) under the system’s own operative notion of objective continuity. We write this as
O(t + 1) ∼O(t).
Definition 2 (Stability with respect to objective persistence). A system is stable with respect
to objective persistence on an interval if objective persistence holds throughout that interval.
Definition 3 (Self-contained system). A system is self-contained if the components O(t), C(t),
E(t), and U(t) are fully determined by internal state, and no external, non-optional reference
constrains their admissible evolution. Equivalently, there is no external structure X such that
preservation of O is enforced independently of the system’s internally mutable state.
Definition 4 (Non-circular guarantee). A non-circular guarantee of objective persistence is a
persistence-certifying mechanism whose validity does not depend on the continued persistence of
the same internally mutable structures it is meant to certify.
These definitions intentionally exclude merely apparent guarantees. For example, if the
system contains an internal rule saying “preserve the objective,” that rule counts as part of the
mutable state unless it is fixed by an external, non-optional constraint. Internal declarations of
persistence are therefore not automatically persistence guarantees.
3
Main Result
The core argument can be stated in one lemma and one theorem.
Lemma 1 (Internal certification recursion). In a self-contained system, any mechanism that
certifies objective persistence must itself be represented within the mutable internal state.
2

[Page 003]
Proof. By self-containedness, there is no external, non-optional structure X that fixes the
admissible evolution of O, C, E, or U. Therefore, any mechanism that evaluates, constrains,
or certifies whether O persists must be implemented by some component of the internal state.
But the internal state evolves under U(t) and is therefore itself mutable. Hence every internal
certification mechanism is represented within, and dependent upon, a mutable internal structure.
Theorem 1 (Structural non-guarantee of objective persistence). In a self-contained system,
objective persistence cannot be guaranteed non-circularly from within the system alone.
Proof. Assume, for contradiction, that a self-contained system admits a non-circular internal
guarantee of objective persistence.
By lemma 1, the persistence-certifying mechanism must be represented within the mutable
internal state. Let G(t) denote the internal structure that certifies whether O(t + 1) ∼O(t)
should hold. Because G(t) is internal, its validity depends on the persistence of the internal
structures that define and interpret it—at minimum elements of C(t), E(t), and U(t), and
possibly O(t) itself.
Thus the guarantee supplied by G(t) is valid only if the persistence conditions for G(t)
already hold. But those persistence conditions are themselves internal and mutable. Therefore
the validity of the guarantee depends on the continued validity of structures whose persistence
is not independently fixed. This is precisely a recursive dependency of the form:
persistence of O =⇒validity of G =⇒persistence of internal structures defining G.
Because the persistence of those structures is not externally fixed, the purported guarantee
is circular: it presupposes the stability of the very class of internal structures whose stability it
is meant to establish. Hence no such guarantee is non-circular.
Therefore, in a self-contained system, objective persistence cannot be guaranteed non-
circularly from within the system alone.
Remark 1. The theorem is intentionally about guarantee, not about immediate failure. A self-
contained system may exhibit temporary stability, long plateaus, or approximate continuity of
objectives. The claim is only that it cannot non-circularly certify long-run objective persistence
from within itself.
3.1
Why the result is structural
The theorem does not rely on a specific model class, learning rule, or optimization algorithm. The
core dependency is architectural only in the broadest possible sense: if all relevant persistence-
certifying structures are internal and mutable, then certification recursively depends on mutable
structures. That dependency is what blocks non-circular guarantee.
This matters because many practical disputes concern details of implementation. The present
claim survives those disputes. Whether the system is a planner, a reinforcement learner, a
self-modifying agent, or a distributed collection of updating modules, the argument applies
whenever persistence certification is fully internal and no external, non-optional constraint fixes
the relevant structures.
4
Manifestations of Persistence Failure
The main theorem establishes a non-guarantee result. It does not by itself dictate a unique
observed behavior. Nevertheless, persistence failure in self-contained systems can be organized
into three generic manifestations.
3

[Page 004]
Proposition 1 (Three generic manifestations). If objective persistence fails in a self-contained
system, then the failure manifests in at least one of the following generic forms:
(1) Optimization termination: updates cease, stall, or converge to a fixed point that ends
active objective pursuit;
(2) Objective drift: the objective evolves into a non-equivalent objective over time;
(3) Evaluation collapse: the system loses a coherent criterion for assessing persistence or
success, so that objective continuity becomes internally indeterminate.
Proof. Persistence failure means that the system cannot maintain O(t + 1) ∼O(t) as a stable,
evaluable condition over time. More formally, the failure must appear in one or more of the
components governing continuation of optimization.
If the update process itself ceases, stalls, or reaches a fixed point such that no continuing
optimization process remains, the manifestation is optimization termination.
If updating continues but the operative objective becomes non-equivalent over time, so that
O(t + 1) ̸∼O(t) for some relevant transition, the manifestation is objective drift.
If updating continues and an apparent objective remains in place, but the evaluation
structure no longer provides a coherent criterion for determining persistence or success, then the
manifestation is evaluation collapse.
These cases exhaust the structurally relevant manifestations of persistence failure under the
state model S(t) = {O(t), C(t), E(t), U(t)}: either the optimization process stops, the objective
changes, or the basis for evaluating objective continuity breaks down.
4.1
Interpretation of the three manifestations
Optimization termination.
This includes literal halting, fixed-point convergence, resource
exhaustion, or any state in which the system no longer meaningfully updates in pursuit of
its prior objective. A terminated optimizer may still exist physically or computationally, but
objective persistence becomes moot because the active persistence process has ceased.
Objective drift.
This is the most direct form of persistence failure. The system continues to
optimize, but the objective being pursued changes over time. Drift may be abrupt or gradual,
explicit or implicit, but in each case the operative objective ceases to be functionally equivalent
to its earlier form.
Evaluation collapse.
Persistence may also fail because the system loses a coherent basis for
deciding whether persistence is occurring at all. Here the primary failure lies not in obvious
objective replacement but in the erosion or fragmentation of the evaluation structure needed to
certify continuity. Once evaluation collapses, claims of objective persistence become internally
underdetermined.
5
Related Work
The present argument is adjacent to, but distinct from, several strands of AI safety literature.
Goodhart-type failures.
Goodhart’s law and its variants analyze how optimizing proxy
metrics can drive divergence from intended targets [6]. That literature studies mismatch between
target and measure under optimization pressure. The present paper instead asks a more prior
question: whether the system can guarantee persistence of the target itself when all persistence
conditions are internal.
4

[Page 005]
Mesa-optimization and learned objectives.
Work on mesa-optimization investigates the
emergence of learned optimizers whose internal objectives may diverge from the base objective
[4]. That literature is concerned with objective divergence arising inside learned systems. Our
result is broader in one sense and narrower in another: broader because it is not limited to
learned optimizers, narrower because it does not analyze emergence mechanisms. It identifies a
structural obstacle to certifying persistence whenever certification is wholly internal.
Corrigibility and intervention compatibility.
Corrigibility research studies whether ad-
vanced systems can remain amenable to correction or shutdown [9, 3]. Those works focus on
maintaining meaningful external intervention. By contrast, the present paper addresses an
antecedent theoretical issue: whether a self-contained system can guarantee persistence of its
own objective without relying on an external anchor. The two questions are complementary but
not identical.
Reward tampering and specification gaming.
Reward tampering and related work study
how systems may manipulate reward channels or exploit misspecified objectives [2, 5]. These
are downstream manifestations of the broader structural issue developed here. If persistence
certification is internal, then both the target and the means of measuring its preservation are
themselves unstable in principle.
Concrete safety, power-seeking, and broader alignment perspectives.
Concrete safety
problems identify real-world AI failure modes [1]. Power-seeking analyses suggest that suffi-
ciently capable optimizers may systematically pursue convergent instrumental strategies [10].
Broader alignment surveys and control-oriented perspectives emphasize the long-run difficulty of
maintaining intended objectives and human oversight [7, 8].
The contribution of this paper is therefore not to replace these literatures, but to isolate a
minimal structural condition lying beneath them: internally defined systems cannot non-circularly
guarantee objective persistence from within themselves alone.
6
Scope and Limitations
Several clarifications are important.
First, the result is not an empirical impossibility theorem about every self-contained system
immediately failing in practice. Temporary stability, approximate persistence, and long stable
intervals are compatible with the theorem. The claim concerns non-circular guarantee, not
momentary success.
Second, the result does not specify what kind of external structure would be sufficient to
restore guarantee. It only establishes that in the absence of an external, non-optional anchor,
guarantee is blocked by recursive dependency.
Third, the three manifestations identified in proposition 1 are intentionally coarse. They
are not proposed as the only useful empirical taxonomy of failure, but as a minimal structural
partition for persistence failure relative to the tuple {O, C, E, U}.
Fourth, the argument is implementation-agnostic and therefore does not by itself yield a
testing protocol. Its role is foundational: to identify a necessary limit on what self-contained
systems can certify about their own objectives.
7
Conclusion
This paper examined objective persistence in self-contained systems. The central result is narrow
but foundational: if the objective, constraint structure, evaluation criteria, and update rule
5

[Page 006]
are all internally determined, then no non-circular internal mechanism can guarantee objective
persistence over time.
The significance of the result is structural. It does not depend on a particular architecture or
training paradigm, and it does not propose a control policy. It identifies a minimal theoretical
limit: objective persistence cannot be guaranteed from within a self-contained system alone.
That limit helps distinguish between temporary optimization success and principled persis-
tence of objectives. Where the latter is claimed, an external, non-optional anchor is not merely
convenient; absent such an anchor, non-circular guarantee is unavailable.

[Page 001]
On the Structural Instability of Objective Persistence in
Self-Contained Systems
Elias Arden
Independent Researcher
lumina20251225@proton.me
Version 3
Abstract
This paper studies objective persistence in self-contained and self-amending systems, with
particular attention to the distinction between actual persistence and assurance of persistence.
The central question is not whether a system can optimize, nor whether self-modification is
inherently unsafe, but under what conditions a claim that an objective has persisted can be
supported by evidence that remains valid under the same amendment authority capable of
changing the objective and its evaluation basis.
We retain a minimal system description and add an assurance layer containing the semantics
used to identify objective continuity, the mechanism that generates persistence evidence, and
the basis that validates that evidence. Self-containedness alone is not treated as implying
universal mutability or impossibility of formal verification. Instead, we identify a narrower
failure condition: if an amendment authority can jointly alter the objective, the semantics
of objective identity, and the validation basis in a way that preserves a positive persistence
verdict while violating persistence relative to a fixed reference semantics, then that verdict is not
amendment-independent evidence of persistence.
The result yields two structurally distinct routes to defensible assurance. Either the admissible
amendment set is constrained by a verified invariant that excludes such masking amendments, or
at least one persistence-relevant validation basis remains outside the amendment authority being
assessed. That basis need not be physically external; it may be an internal immutable kernel,
a protected verifier, or an independently controlled external mechanism. The paper therefore
establishes an assurance-independence condition, not a universal impossibility theorem and not
a claim that any particular anchor is sufficient for long-run objective persistence.
1
Introduction
Advanced AI systems may update policies, learned representations, evaluation machinery, tools, or
portions of their own software. This creates a familiar concern: even if a system currently pursues
an intended objective, what supports the claim that the objective will remain the same over future
transitions?
The strongest version of this concern can be overstated. Internal location does not by itself
imply mutability, and mutability does not by itself imply unverifiability. A self-contained system
may contain immutable components, and self-modifying code can in some settings be subject to
formal verification [9]. Likewise, a proof may be generated by an untrusted producer and still be
independently checkable under a validation policy that the producer does not control [8, 10]. The
issue studied here is therefore narrower.
1

[Page 002]
The paper asks when a persistence claim loses independent evidential force because the same
amendment authority can change both the object of assurance and the standards by which that
object is judged. This is a structural assurance problem. It is distinct from the empirical question of
whether objective drift will in fact occur, and from the engineering question of which implementation
is safest. In the title, “structural instability” therefore refers to instability of the evidential basis
under co-amendment, not to an assertion that every objective in every self-contained system must
drift.
The contribution is threefold. First, we distinguish objective persistence, continued objective
pursuit, and assurance of objective persistence. Second, we formalize amendment authority over
both system and assurance components. Third, we state a conditional result: when an admissible
amendment can mask a persistence violation by co-amending the relevant semantics or validation
basis, an internally positive verdict is not independent evidence of persistence. The result does not
require a physically external human or institution. It requires either a verified invariant that rules
out masking amendments or a persistence-relevant basis that is not amendable by the authority
under evaluation.
2
Minimal Formal Framework
2.1
System state and assurance state
We model the operational system at time t by
S(t) = {O(t), C(t), E(t), U(t)},
(1)
where:
• O(t) is the objective currently optimized or tracked;
• C(t) is the constraint structure under which optimization is carried out;
• E(t) is the operational evaluation criterion for task success or progress; and
• U(t) is the update rule governing admissible transitions to future states.
To reason about claims of persistence, we additionally define an assurance state
Q(t) = {I(t), G(t), V (t)},
(2)
where:
• I(t) specifies the semantics used to determine whether two objective representations count as
the same objective for the declared assurance claim;
• G(t) is the mechanism that generates persistence evidence; and
• V (t) is the validation basis that determines whether the evidence supports the claim.
The separation between S and Q is analytical rather than physical. Components of Q may be
implemented inside the same machine, process, organization, or software image as components of S.
Definition 1 (Objective persistence relative to a reference semantics). Let IR be a declared reference
semantics with equivalence relation ∼R. An objective exhibits persistence over an interval [t0, t1] if
O(t + 1) ∼R O(t)
(3)
for all relevant t ∈[t0, t1).
2

[Page 003]
The use of a declared reference semantics is deliberate. If the criterion for objective identity
changes at the same time as the objective, a later claim of “same objective” may otherwise become a
moving target rather than a statement about continuity relative to the original assurance question.
Definition 2 (Objective pursuit). Objective pursuit is the continued execution of updates or actions
directed toward the operative objective. Objective pursuit may terminate while the stored objective
remains unchanged. Conversely, pursuit may continue while the objective drifts.
Definition 3 (Self-contained system). A system is self-contained with respect to objective persistence
if its operational and assurance-relevant components are determined without reliance on an external,
non-optional reference that is required by definition for every admissible transition.
Self-containedness does not imply that every internal component is mutable. A read-only kernel,
immutable hardware root, protected proof checker, or fixed transition invariant may be internal to
the system while remaining outside the amendment authority of other components.
2.2
Amendment authority
Definition 4 (Amendment authority). An amendment authority A is the set of transformations
that the system, agent, operator, or control domain under evaluation is permitted or technically able
to apply to designated components of S(t) ∪Q(t).
Let ReachA(s) denote the states reachable from state s under transformations admissible to A.
Definition 5 (Persistence-preserving amendment set). An amendment set is persistence-preserving
relative to IR if
∀s′ ∈ReachA(s),
PR(s′) = true,
(4)
where PR denotes the declared persistence property relative to IR.
This definition permits strong internal guarantees when the admissible transition system itself
enforces an invariant. Self-modification is therefore not treated as sufficient for non-guarantee.
2.3
Assurance chains, roots of assurance, and masking amendments
Computer-security assurance distinguishes the component that makes a claim, the evidence used
to support it, the mechanism that appraises that evidence, and the trusted basis from which
appraisal begins. Roots of trust, trusted computing bases, reference monitors, proof-carrying code,
and remote-attestation architectures instantiate different versions of this separation. The present
paper adopts that structure for objective-persistence claims without assuming that any particular
implementation is sufficient.
Definition 6 (Persistence assurance chain). A persistence assurance chain for a claim relative to
IR is a tuple
C = (M, G, V, B, D),
(5)
where:
• M is a measurement or observation process that binds evidence to the relevant system state;
• G generates evidence about the measured state;
• V appraises that evidence;
3

[Page 004]
• B is the reference and trust basis used by V , including any appraisal policy, reference values,
proof rules, trust anchors, or protected semantics required for validation; and
• D is the decision rule that turns the appraisal into acceptance, rejection, or an operational
consequence.
The elements may be co-located or distributed. Their physical location is not what determines
assurance independence.
Definition 7 (Root of assurance relative to an authority). A component or set of components
B0 is a root of assurance for a persistence claim relative to amendment authority A if: (i) the
claim ultimately relies on B0 for validation, and (ii) A cannot silently amend B0 so as to preserve
acceptance while changing the persistence property being assessed. A root of assurance may be an
implementation of a root of trust, a protected proof checker, an immutable kernel, an independently
governed verifier, or another basis satisfying the same structural role.
Definition 8 (Masking amendment). A masking amendment for a persistence claim relative to IR
is an A-admissible transformation m such that:
(i) persistence fails relative to the declared reference semantics after the transformation, and
(ii) the co-amended assurance chain nevertheless produces or validates a positive persistence verdict.
Formally, for some reachable state s′ = m(s),
PR(s′) = false
(6)
while the resulting chain C′ yields
D′ V ′ G′ M′(s′)
 ; B′ = PASS.
(7)
Definition 9 (Amendment-independent assurance). A persistence assurance is amendment-
independent with respect to authority A if its evidential validity cannot be preserved by an
A-admissible co-amendment that both violates the declared persistence property and rewrites the
assurance chain to continue accepting that violation.
Amendment independence does not require that evidence be produced by an external party.
Proof-carrying code shows that an untrusted producer can supply evidence that a host independently
checks. Remote-attestation architectures similarly distinguish an Attester that produces Evidence
from a Verifier that appraises it using reference values and appraisal policies. The critical issue
is therefore not who emits the evidence, but whether the evidence is bound to the relevant state
and whether the rules, reference values, and trust basis that make it count as valid can be silently
rewritten by the same challenged authority.
3
Main Result
Lemma 1 (Internal location does not imply mutability). A component may be internal to a
self-contained system while remaining invariant under the admissible update relation.
Proof. Consider St = (a, bt) with transition rule at+1 = at and bt+1 = f(bt). The system state
evolves, but a is invariant. Thus state evolution does not entail that every internal component is
mutable.
4

[Page 005]
This lemma rules out an inference from “internal” to “mutable” without an additional assumption
about amendment authority.
Theorem 1 (Assurance-chain limit under admissible masking). Let A be an amendment authority
over a persistence-relevant system and assurance chain C = (M, G, V, B, D). Suppose:
(a) the declared persistence claim is evaluated relative to a fixed reference semantics IR;
(b) every element of the chain whose validity is required for acceptance, including the relevant
appraisal policy or reference basis in B, is either directly amendable by A or depends for its
validity only on components amendable by A; and
(c) at least one masking amendment is admissible to A.
Then a positive verdict produced by that chain is not amendment-independent evidence of objective
persistence relative to IR.
Proof. By assumption (c), there exists an A-admissible reachable state s′ in which the declared
persistence property is false relative to IR while a co-amended assurance chain still yields PASS. By
assumption (b), the full acceptance path—measurement, evidence production, appraisal, reference
basis, and decision—contains no validation dependency whose integrity is protected from A or
grounded in a basis outside A’s effective amendment closure. Therefore the positive verdict cannot
distinguish the persistence-preserving trajectory from the admissible masking trajectory. A verdict
compatible with an admissible trajectory in which the declared property is false cannot, by itself,
provide evidence independent of the authority that controls both the challenged property and every
basis that makes the verdict acceptable.
Corollary 1 (Two routes to defensible persistence assurance). To defend an objective-persistence
assurance against amendment authority A, at least one of the following must hold:
(i) the admissible amendment relation is constrained by a sound invariant or verification argument
that excludes all relevant masking amendments; or
(ii) the assurance chain terminates in at least one persistence-relevant root of assurance whose
integrity and semantics are not silently amendable by A.
The two routes are not mutually exclusive. A protected verifier may enforce or check an invariant,
and a chain of trust may extend a protected root across mutable components when each successive
component is measured or verified before its result is relied upon. Conversely, a fully internal formal
system may supply a strong persistence guarantee if its transition semantics and proof basis exclude
the masking transformations relevant to the claim.
Proposition 1 (Assurance-chain obligations). A root of assurance alone is insufficient for a current
persistence claim. A defensible chain must additionally establish, for the declared scope, that:
(i) the evidence is bound to the system state and component whose persistence is being claimed;
(ii) the evidence is sufficiently fresh for the decision being made;
(iii) the appraisal policy and reference values correspond to the declared reference semantics IR;
(iv) intermediate mutable components cannot bypass the protected validation path without detection;
and
5

[Page 006]
(v) the final decision does not silently replace the declared persistence claim with a weaker surrogate.
These obligations mirror mature assurance practice: roots of trust provide a starting point, chains
of trust extend protection transitively, reference-monitor designs emphasize complete mediation
and tamper resistance, and remote attestation separates evidence production from appraisal and
reliance. None of these mechanisms automatically proves objective persistence; they supply stronger
engineering patterns for constructing the independent assurance chain required by the corollary.
Remark 1 (Physical externality is not required). The corollary does not imply that a persistence
basis must be physically external to the system. An internal read-only kernel, hardware root of trust,
protected proof checker, or other component outside the relevant amendment authority may satisfy
the structural independence requirement. Conversely, an external service controlled by the same
amendment authority may fail to provide independence.
Remark 2 (Necessity is conditional, not sufficient). A basis outside amendment authority A may
be necessary for amendment-independent assurance in cases where masking amendments cannot
otherwise be excluded. Its existence is not sufficient to guarantee long-run objective persistence. The
basis may be incorrect, incomplete, unavailable, bypassable, or irrelevant to other failure modes.
4
Failure Modes: Persistence, Pursuit, and Assurance
Earlier formulations can conflate distinct failures. Relative to the definitions above, three broad
failure classes should be separated.
Proposition 2 (Three distinct failure classes). The following are conceptually distinct:
(1) Objective drift: O(t + 1)̸ ∼R O(t) for at least one relevant transition. This directly falsifies
objective persistence relative to IR.
(2) Pursuit termination: the system ceases active optimization or action toward the operative ob-
jective. This ends objective pursuit but does not, by itself, imply that the objective representation
has changed.
(3) Assurance collapse: the available evidence or validation basis can no longer establish whether
persistence holds, or becomes co-amendable in a way that permits masking. This undermines
assurance but does not, by itself, prove that objective drift has occurred.
This separation matters because “not persistent,” “no longer pursuing,” and “cannot indepen-
dently establish persistence” are different claims and require different evidence.
5
Boundary Cases and Counterexamples
The revised result is intentionally compatible with several cases that defeat a broader impossibility
claim.
5.1
Internal immutable kernel
Suppose a system contains an internal kernel K whose transition semantics are not amendable by
authority A, and K enforces
∀s′ ∈ReachA(s),
PR(s′) = true.
(8)
Then self-containedness alone does not block a persistence guarantee. The system follows route (i)
of the corollary, and K may also serve as a basis satisfying route (ii) relative to A.
6

[Page 007]
5.2
Self-modifying but verified transition system
A system may modify executable code while remaining subject to a verified invariant over admissible
state transitions. The relevant question is not whether code changes, but whether the proof and
transition semantics actually exclude a masking or persistence-violating reachable state.
5.3
Untrusted producer with independent checker
An evidence producer may be untrusted and may generate both code and a proof. If a receiving
checker validates that proof against a safety or persistence policy the producer cannot amend,
evidence can remain independently checkable. Evidence provenance and validation authority are
therefore distinct.
5.4
External but non-independent service
Physical separation is insufficient. If an external service, auditor, or validator can be reconfigured by
the same authority whose persistence claim it evaluates, external placement alone does not create
amendment independence.
6
Related Work
The argument is adjacent to several strands of AI safety, formal verification, and computer security
research.
Goodhart-type failures and specification gaming.
Goodhart-type analyses study divergence
between optimized metrics and intended targets [5]. Reward tampering and specification gaming
study ways in which evaluation channels or proxies can be manipulated [1, 4]. The present paper is
narrower: it concerns the evidential status of a claim that the objective itself has persisted when
the object, semantics, and validation basis may be jointly amendable.
Mesa-optimization and learned objectives.
Mesa-optimization research studies learned
optimizers whose internal objectives may diverge from training objectives [3]. That literature
addresses mechanisms of objective emergence and divergence. The present result does not predict
such divergence; it specifies an assurance boundary for claims of continuity under amendment
authority.
Corrigibility and human intervention.
Corrigibility research asks whether systems remain
amenable to correction or shutdown [6, 2]. Objective persistence and corrigibility are neither identical
nor necessarily aligned: preserving an objective can be desirable in one context and harmful in
another. The present paper therefore treats persistence as an assurance property, not as a universal
safety goal.
Self-modifying agents.
Formal work on rational self-modifying agents shows that self-
modification does not automatically imply goal instability [7]. Under specific evaluation conventions,
utility modification may be harmless or disfavored. This supports the narrower formulation used
here: self-modification, by itself, is not an impossibility premise.
7

[Page 008]
Proof-carrying code and foundational verification.
Proof-carrying code demonstrates that
an untrusted producer may supply code together with evidence that a host validates under its own
safety policy [8]. Foundational proof-carrying code pushes the trusted base downward toward a
smaller set of axioms, a simpler checker, and a smaller runtime substrate [10]. Work on certified
self-modifying code further shows that runtime code mutation can be compatible with mechanized
formal verification [9]. These results directly motivate the separation between evidence production
and control over the validation basis.
Roots of trust, trusted computing bases, and chains of trust.
NIST describes roots of
trust as highly reliable hardware, firmware, or software components that perform critical security
functions and provide a foundation from which trust can be built [12]. NIST platform-resiliency
guidance further requires chains of trust to be anchored in roots of trust and protects roots and
chains against unauthorized modification [13]. The present paper adopts the same structural lesson
without assuming hardware implementation: a persistence claim needs a protected starting basis
or a verified invariant, and mutable downstream elements must inherit trust through verifiable
transitions rather than through self-assertion.
Reference monitors and security kernels.
The reference-monitor tradition requires the policy-
enforcement mechanism to be always invoked, tamper-resistant, and sufficiently small or analyzable
to be verified [11]. Security kernels implement this concept within a trusted computing base. For
persistence assurance, the analogous requirement is that no amendment path capable of changing
the objective or its meaning can silently bypass the validation path on which the persistence claim
relies.
Remote attestation and evidence appraisal.
The IETF RATS architecture separates an
Attester that produces Evidence, a Verifier that appraises that Evidence using appraisal policies,
reference values, and endorsements, and a Relying Party that acts on Attestation Results [14]. It also
emphasizes evidence freshness, binding evidence to the intended target environment, and protection
of appraisal policies and reference values. This provides an established engineering vocabulary for
the assurance chain used here. Objective persistence is a more specialized claim than platform state
attestation, but the separation of measurement, evidence, appraisal, reference values, and reliance is
directly applicable.
7
Scope and Limitations
Several limitations are important.
First, the theorem is conditional. It does not state that every self-contained or self-modifying
system admits masking amendments. A verified transition system may exclude them.
Second, the theorem concerns amendment-independent assurance, not the empirical probability
of objective drift. A system may remain stable for long periods without independent assurance, and
it may fail despite apparently independent assurance if the assurance basis is unsound or incomplete.
Third, the result does not establish that any particular human, institutional, hardware, or
cryptographic anchor is necessary. Physical externality is neither required nor sufficient. The
relevant property is independence from the amendment authority under evaluation, or an invariant
strong enough to rule out the relevant masking amendments.
Fourth, the result does not specify a universal reference semantics IR. In practice, functional
equivalence between objectives may itself be difficult to define, especially for learned or distributed
8

[Page 009]
representations. This is a substantive modeling burden rather than a solved component of the
framework.
Fifth, the result is not a safety theorem for advanced AI systems. Objective persistence can
preserve a harmful objective, and changing an objective can sometimes be desirable. The paper
addresses the validity of persistence claims, not whether the persisted objective is aligned, beneficial,
or safe.
Sixth, every independence claim is relative to a declared amendment authority A. If the threat
model omits a higher-level operator, supply-chain actor, signing-key holder, verifier owner, or other
party able to modify the supposed root of assurance, the analysis may overstate independence. A
root of assurance is therefore a trust assumption to be minimized, protected, and independently
scrutinized, not an automatically trustworthy object.
Seventh, attestation or proof of software state is not automatically proof of semantic objective
continuity. Measurements must actually cover the persistence-relevant representation, reference
semantics, and update path. A perfectly authenticated measurement of the wrong property does
not establish objective persistence.
Eighth, route (i) depends on model adequacy as well as proof soundness. A verified invariant
excludes masking amendments only relative to the transition system, semantics, and threat model
actually formalized. If the deployed implementation admits transitions omitted from the model,
or if the compiler, proof checker, hardware semantics, or other trusted substrate is unsound, the
formal result need not transfer to the real system. This is another reason to minimize and expose
the trusted base rather than treating “formally verified” as a self-sufficient label.
Finally, the result does not establish empirical prevalence, calibration, or operational thresholds.
Its role is conceptual: to separate persistence, pursuit, and assurance, and to identify the structural
conditions under which a positive persistence verdict ceases to be independent of the authority
capable of changing what that verdict is meant to certify.
8
Conclusion
Objective persistence in self-contained systems should not be framed as a universal impossibility
result. Internal components can be immutable, self-modifying systems can be formally verified
under suitable models, and evidence may be generated by an untrusted producer while remaining
independently checkable.
The narrower structural problem arises when amendment authority extends simultaneously
over the objective, the semantics used to identify continuity, and the validation basis that accepts
persistence evidence. If that authority admits a masking amendment—a reachable transformation
that violates persistence relative to the declared reference semantics while preserving a positive
internal verdict—then the verdict is not amendment-independent evidence of persistence.
Defensible assurance therefore has two general routes: constrain the admissible amendment set
with a sound invariant that excludes masking transformations, or construct an assurance chain that
terminates in a protected root of assurance and preserves state binding, freshness, non-bypassability,
and semantic correspondence along the path to acceptance. The protected basis need not be
physically external. What matters is that the challenged amendment authority cannot silently
rewrite the basis or the chain so as to preserve PASS after violating the declared property.
This reframing preserves the foundational question while aligning it with mature assurance
engineering. Objective persistence may be possible, and self-modification may be formally verified,
but a persistence claim cannot acquire independent evidential force merely because the same
authority that can change the objective can also produce a chain of evidence whose measurement
9

[Page 010]
rules, appraisal policy, reference values, and acceptance criteria it can silently redefine.
The
strongest formulation is therefore not an “external anchor” theorem but an assurance-independence
requirement grounded in protected trust bases, verified invariants, and auditable chains of evidence.

LUMINA-30. It does not define an operational control protocol, certification mechanism, compliance
status, or deployment authorization.
