[Page 001]
Pre-Irreversibility Safety for Advanced AI
Distributed Irreversible Authority, Timely Refusal,
Human-Revisable Development
Elias Arden
Independent Researcher
lumina20251225@proton.me
Version 1.2 – October 10, 2026
Concept DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23042905
Abstract
Advanced-AI safety spans alignment, capability evaluation, control, monitoring, human
oversight, governance, and systemic-risk analysis. Existing work already treats irreversibility,
distributed trust, meaningful human control, runtime assurance, gradual human disempowerment,
viability, and safety cases. The contribution attempted here is therefore not priority over those
ideas, but a stronger operational composition for claims about whether a socio-technical system
remains human-revisable before irreversible transitions.
The Pre-Irreversibility Safety Framework (PISF) is organized around three analytically
separable candidate claims. Distributed Irreversible Authority asks whether any tolerated
coalition of human or artificial authority domains, after accounting for common dependencies
and the currently reachable state envelope, can force a transition into an irreversible region.
Effective Pre-Irreversibility Refusal asks whether a causally effective refusal can take effect
before a conservative last-safe-intervention bound; it distinguishes the latency of restraint from
the latency of verifying that restraint. Human-Revisable Development Viability asks
whether human-governed policies can robustly keep development inside a declared revisability
region while preserving more than a single path-locked future.
The framework associates these claims with: (i) a family of minimal irreversible authority cut
sets, with cardinality and compromise-cost summaries; (ii) a robust causal refusal margin; and
(iii) a finite-horizon human-revisable viability kernel augmented by an optionality measure over
materially distinct reachable futures. Boundary Completeness, Model Adequacy, and Independent
Assurance are treated separately as conditions governing whether evidence for the three claims
is credible. Evidence is classified as Verified, Unresolved, or Failed; unresolved evidence is
not treated as a pass.
The paper does not claim that Authority, Refusal, and Revisability form a unique or mathe-
matically minimal decomposition, nor that satisfying them is sufficient for safe superintelligence.
Counterexamples support only their diagnostic separability under the declared definitions.
Worked toy cases show how the revised objects can be recomputed, including a cut-set example,
a refusal-timing example that separates causal effect from verification, and a viability/optionality
example. They do not constitute empirical validation of frontier-AI deployments.
Keywords: Pre-Irreversibility Safety Framework; PISF; distributed irreversible authority; minimal
cut sets; timely refusal; runtime assurance; human-revisable development; viability; future optionality;
meaningful human control; independent assurance; AI control
1

[Page 002]
1
Introduction
AI safety increasingly operates across several layers. Alignment research asks whether systems
pursue intended objectives. AI control asks whether harmful outcomes can still be prevented when
an untrusted model may actively attempt subversion. Frontier safety frameworks connect capability
thresholds to safeguards and deployment governance. Meaningful-human-control research asks when
human oversight is substantive rather than nominal. Safety-case research asks whether high-level
safety claims can be supported by explicit arguments and evidence. System-safety and viability
research address control, failure propagation, safe sets, and recoverability in complex systems
[13, 24, 10, 14, 16, 6].
Irreversibility is not a new safety concern.
Small-coalition vulnerability, unilateral action,
distributed trust, runtime safety enforcement, and gradual disempowerment each have substantial
prior literatures [8, 9, 20, 17, 15]. The narrower question addressed here is:
Can claims about pre-irreversibility human control be decomposed into
operational objects that third parties can inspect, recompute, challenge, and
revise without assuming that any one actor, model, or evaluator remains
permanently correct?
Three recurrent failure structures motivate the decomposition. First, a small coalition may hold
enough effective authority to force an irreversible transition even when monitoring and nominal
oversight exist. Second, refusal may exist formally yet take effect too late. Third, no actor may
deliberately cross a catastrophic boundary, yet ordinary development can progressively remove
fallback capacity, institutional competence, infrastructure diversity, or materially distinct future
choices.
PISF therefore uses the diagnostic decomposition
PISF claim = A ∧B ∧C,
(1)
where A is Distributed Irreversible Authority, B is Effective Pre-Irreversibility Refusal, and C is
Human-Revisable Development Viability. This notation is a safety-case organization, not a theorem
that A ∧B ∧C is sufficient for safe advanced AI, and not a claim that these are the unique or
minimal safety dimensions.
Version 1.2 makes five substantive changes to the earlier formulation. First, Core A is re-expressed
using state-conditioned minimal irreversible cut sets rather than treating domain count alone as the
central object. Second, Core B separates causal restraint latency from assurance latency. Third,
Core C separates robust viability from preservation of materially distinct future options. Fourth,
evidence for all three cores is placed behind a stronger independent-assurance condition informed by
roots-of-trust and attestation architectures. Fifth, the paper explicitly treats A/B/C as a revisable
diagnostic decomposition rather than a uniquely established ontology of pre-irreversibility safety.
2
Related Work
2.1
AI control and adversarial safety
Greenblatt et al. define AI control as a second layer of defense in which protocols aim to prevent
harmful outcomes even if an untrusted model attempts intentional subversion [13]. This is directly
relevant to both Core A and Core B: authority structures must not be bypassable by the system
being constrained, and refusal paths should remain effective under the threat model rather than
only during benign operation.
2

[Page 003]
2.2
Meaningful human control and oversight
Santoni de Sio and van den Hoven argue that meaningful human control cannot be reduced to
a human merely being “in the loop” and develop tracking and tracing conditions linking system
behavior to relevant human reasons and responsibility [24]. More recent work on medical AI
similarly argues that oversight is meaningful only when users have epistemic capacity, cognitive
space, decisional authority, and practical intervention effectiveness [25]. These works motivate
treating human authority, information, time, and intervention effectiveness as operational rather
than nominal properties.
2.3
Distributed trust, cut sets, and threshold authorization
Threshold cryptography distributes security-critical operations across multiple parties so that
compromise of a bounded subset need not compromise the protected operation [20]. Fault-tree
analysis uses minimal cut sets to represent minimal combinations of component failures sufficient
to cause a top event [19]. PISF borrows the structural idea of minimal cut sets but applies it to
socio-technical authority: a cut set is a minimal coalition whose effective authority can force an
irreversible transition under a declared threat realization. The analogy is useful but not exact; PISF
authority domains can include people, organizations, credentials, infrastructure, and institutional
procedures.
2.4
Reachability, barrier functions, and viability
Hamilton–Jacobi reachability provides formal tools for analyzing whether nonlinear or hybrid
systems can reach or avoid unsafe sets under control and disturbance [11]. Control barrier functions
characterize forward invariance of safe sets and support real-time safety constraints [1]. Viability
theory studies states from which some admissible policy can keep a system inside a constraint set [6].
PISF does not claim novelty over these control-theoretic objects. It uses their structure to sharpen
questions about human-governed intervention and future revisability in socio-technical AI systems.
2.5
Runtime assurance
The Simplex family of runtime-assurance architectures separates an advanced controller from
a trusted or verified reversionary mechanism and transfers control when safety conditions are
threatened. Black-Box Simplex provides a peer-reviewed formalization in which runtime checks
support safety without requiring the advanced controller itself to be verified [17]. NASA has also
described runtime assurance as a route for integrating AI/ML or otherwise unverified software into
safety-critical systems [18]. PISF treats runtime assurance as one important implementation class
for Core B, not as a complete substitute for civilization-scale authority and revisability analysis.
2.6
Gradual disempowerment and systemic risk
Kulveit et al. analyze gradual disempowerment in which incremental AI adoption can weaken human
influence across economic, cultural, and state systems without requiring an abrupt takeover [15].
This is a strong conceptual predecessor to Core C. PISF therefore does not treat gradual erosion of
human influence as a novel discovery; it asks whether such trajectories can be represented as loss of
robust human-revisability and loss of future optionality.
3

[Page 004]
2.7
Safety cases and independent assurance
Frontier-AI safety-case work proposes explicit claims, arguments, assumptions, and evidence as a
scalable basis for safety review [10, 14]. NIST SP 800-193 distinguishes roots of trust and chains
of trust used to protect, detect, and recover platform state [21]. RFC 9334 separates an Attester,
Evidence, a Verifier, an appraisal policy, and a Relying Party [23]. PISF uses these as mature
assurance precedents: evidence about A/B/C should not be considered independently credible if the
evaluated authority can freely modify the evidence source, appraisal rule, verifier, and acceptance
decision while preserving a nominal pass.
2.8
Frontier-AI risk frameworks
Anthropic’s Responsible Scaling Policy, Google DeepMind’s Frontier Safety Framework, OpenAI’s
Preparedness Framework, and the International AI Safety Report provide evolving capability,
safeguard, and risk-management structures [2, 12, 22, 7]. PISF is not a competing risk taxonomy.
Evidence produced by such frameworks can feed the narrower A/B/C claims.
3
Problem Formulation
3.1
Analysis specification
Every PISF claim is conditional on a declared analysis specification
Σ = (B, Θ, R, H),
(2)
where B is the modeled socio-technical boundary, Θ the threat/failure model, R the human-
revisability specification, and H the evaluation horizon. Application-specific parameters may also
include an authority-tolerance class TA, an intervention safety buffer ∆safe > 0, and optionality
parameters (m, δ) defined below.
Let xt ∈X denote the socio-technical state. The modeled boundary may include AI systems and
agents, humans and organizations, credentials, compute, network and cloud dependencies, physical
actuators, monitors, logs, safety mechanisms, and institutional rules. Abstract dynamics may be
written as
xt+1 = F(xt, uH
t , uS
t , dt),
(3)
where uH
t denotes actions permitted by the human-governed control structure, uS
t other system or
actor actions, and dt disturbances or environmental changes. The symbols do not imply that these
categories are perfectly separable in practice; they force the analysis to declare who can control
what.
3.2
Revisability and irreversibility
Let VR ⊆X denote states satisfying the declared revisability specification. Such a specification may
include continued human-governed authority over consequential changes, independent information
channels, fallback capability, ability to replace or disable critical AI services, infrastructure diversity,
and preservation of materially distinct feasible alternatives.
A temporary exit from VR need not be irreversible. Define IR ⊆X as the states from which no
feasible human-governed policy can restore the declared revisability specification within the stated
time, resource, authority, and physical constraints. Both VR and IR are specification-relative and
must not be silently treated as universal moral truths.
4

[Page 005]
4
Core A: Distributed Irreversible Authority
4.1
Authority domains and dependency closure
Let the primitive authority domains be
A = {a1, . . . , an}.
(4)
An authority domain is a set of permissions, credentials, infrastructure, procedures, or decision rights
treated as jointly compromisable under Θ. Numerical plurality is not independence: a thousand
model instances that depend on one root administrator, identity provider, or release credential may
still form a single effective authority domain.
For threat realization θ ∈Θ and coalition K ⊆A, let Clθ(K) denote the dependency closure
induced by compromise or capture of K.
4.2
Reachable-envelope forcing predicate
The previous formulation counted a coalition as irreversible-forcing if it could force irreversibility
from some relevant state. Version 1.2 binds this claim to the evaluated state x0 and horizon H.
Let Reachθ(x0, H) denote the states reachable from x0 within the declared horizon under the
modeled admissible evolution for threat realization θ, before crediting coalition K with the irreversible
forcing step whose existence is being tested. The envelope may include ordinary operation, modeled
disturbances, and strategic behavior already admitted by Θ; it must not be inflated by assuming
the very irreversible transition that IFθ is meant to diagnose. Define
IFθ(K | x0, H) = 1
(5)
iff there exists x ∈Reachθ(x0, H) such that authority available through Clθ(K) is sufficient to
force a trajectory into IR without the active cooperation of an uncompromised authority domain.
Otherwise IFθ(K | x0, H) = 0.
The word “force” is threat-model relative. If Θ includes strategic resistance by uncompromised
domains, IFθ = 1 requires the coalition to succeed despite that modeled resistance; if such resistance
has not been modeled, the stronger forcing claim remains unresolved rather than silently assumed.
This state-conditioning prevents a remote or unreachable hypothetical state from automatically
lowering the current authority threshold.
4.3
Minimal irreversible authority cut sets
Define the minimal cut-set family
CE(θ | x0, H) =
K ⊆A : IFθ(K | x0, H) = 1, IFθ(K′ | x0, H) = 0 ∀K′ ⊊K
	 .
(6)
Two summaries are useful but should not replace the cut-set family itself. The minimum cardinality
is
κE = inf
θ∈Θ
inf
K∈CE(θ|x0,H) |K|,
(7)
with κE = ∞if no modeled cut set exists. If a threat model supplies a coalition compromise cost
cθ(K), define
χE = inf
θ∈Θ
inf
K∈CE(θ|x0,H) cθ(K).
(8)
Cardinality and cost answer different questions. A one-domain root credential and a one-domain
low-privilege reviewer are not automatically equivalent merely because both have cardinality one.
5

[Page 006]
4.4
Core-A condition
Let TA(θ) denote the declared class of coalitions the design claims to tolerate. Core A requires
∀θ ∈Θ, ∀K ∈TA(θ) :
IFθ(K | x0, H) = 0.
(9)
If TA(θ) = {K : |K| ≤f}, this reduces to the earlier summary condition κE > f. The more
general statement makes explicit that cardinality is one tolerance model, not a universal measure of
authority risk.
5
Core B: Effective Pre-Irreversibility Refusal
5.1
Executable refusal
For state x and hazard h, let UR(x, h) denote practically executable refusal actions, including
pause, STOP, isolation, rollback, credential revocation, resource denial, degraded safe operation, or
redirection. A nominal right or user-interface control is insufficient unless it can causally alter the
relevant trajectory.
5.2
Causal effect latency and assurance latency
Version 1.1 included verification time in the same latency scalar as detection, decision, authorization,
and enforcement. That conflated two questions: when restraint takes effect, and when an evaluator
can confirm that it took effect.
At evaluation or trigger time t0, define τeffect(h) as the remaining latency until effective causal
restraint of hazard h. If the relevant trigger has not yet been detected, the bound must include the
remaining detection delay; after a pre-authorized trigger has fired, already-completed steps are not
counted again. Its critical path may include detection, decision, authorization, and enforcement,
while pre-authorized machine-speed policies can move some steps outside the runtime critical path.
Define τassure(h) as the remaining latency from t0 to obtain evidence that the restraint has taken
effect and remains within the declared boundary.
In general,
τeffect(h) ≤τassure(h),
(10)
but neither equality nor strict inequality is universal.
5.3
Robust last-safe-intervention margin
Let T L
I (h) denote a conservative lower bound on the last time by which effective restraint must
occur for hazard h, and let τ U
effect(h) denote a conservative upper bound on causal effect latency. At
current time t0 define
MR(h) = T L
I (h) −t0 −τ U
effect(h),
(11)
and for declared hazard set H,
Mrob
R
= inf
h∈H MR(h).
(12)
Core B requires an executable refusal policy for each relevant hazard and
Mrob
R
> ∆safe > 0.
(13)
This is a causal-control condition. A separate assurance condition determines whether the
evidence for τ U
effect, T L
I , and successful enforcement is independently credible.
6

[Page 007]
5.4
Human-defined machine-speed refusal
Core B does not require a human to react manually on machine timescales. Humans may pre-
authorize a rule
q ⇒πR
(14)
that triggers refusal policy πR automatically, provided that: (i) the boundary and rule remain subject
to legitimate human-governed revision; (ii) the enforcing mechanism is sufficiently independent of
the constrained system for the declared threat model; and (iii) the enforcement mechanism does not
thereby acquire unilateral irreversible-GO authority. Runtime-assurance and Simplex architectures
are important implementation classes for such structures [17, 18].
6
Core C: Human-Revisable Development Viability
6.1
Robust viability
Core C addresses a different failure path: every local action may appear permissible and acute
hazards may remain stoppable, yet ordinary development can progressively remove human ability
to pursue materially different futures.
Let NΘ denote the modeled set of admissible non-human, adversarial, institutional, market,
and environmental trajectories under the declared threat model. Define the finite-horizon robust
human-revisable viability kernel
KH
R = {x0 ∈VR : ∃πH ∀ν ∈NΘ, xt ∈VR ∀t ∈[0, H]} ,
(15)
where πH is a policy permitted by the human-governed institutional structure. Automated controls
are allowed if the relevant constitutional authority remains human-revisable.
Exact civilization-scale kernels are unlikely to be computable. In practice, KH
R will require
conservative approximations, scenario families, reachable-set bounds, barrier certificates, simulations,
or surrogate indicators. PISF treats that limitation as part of the evidence state rather than silently
converting an approximation into certainty.
6.2
Future optionality
Remaining inside a safe or revisable set does not by itself show that meaningful choice remains. A
state can be viable yet path-locked to a single future corridor.
Let g : X →Y project system states into an application-specific outcome space with metric dY .
For separation threshold δ > 0, define Ωδ
H(x0) as the maximum number of human-governed policies
that can, under the declared threat model, robustly preserve VR while reaching pairwise δ-separated
terminal outcome regions at horizon H.
The exact construction of Ωδ
H is application-specific. Its role is diagnostic: Ωδ
H = 1 may certify
survival of a single robust corridor while failing to support a stronger claim that materially distinct
human-governed futures remain available.
For an application declaring a required option count m ≥1, Core C requires
x0 ∈KH
R
and
Ωδ
H(x0) ≥m.
(16)
When m = 1, the second condition adds nothing beyond viability. Larger m represents an explicit,
contestable requirement for preserved future optionality rather than an implicit appeal to “human
agency.”
7

[Page 008]
7
Analytic Separability, Not Uniqueness
The framework does not claim that A, B, and C are statistically independent, ontologically funda-
mental, or a uniquely minimal decomposition. The narrower claim is diagnostic separability under
the declared definitions.
A can fail while B and C hold.
Refusal may be rapid and development may remain revisable,
yet one root authority can bypass all safeguards and force irreversible release.
B can fail while A and C hold.
Irreversible action may require several independent authorities
and normal development may remain revisable, yet the hazard can cross the last safe intervention
boundary faster than restraint can take effect.
C can fail while A and B hold.
No tolerated coalition may be able to force irreversible action
and emergency refusal may remain fast, yet normal deployment can erode fallback infrastructure,
human competence, or materially distinct future options.
These examples establish non-implication in the model class. They do not prove that A/B/C
are the only useful decomposition. If a smaller or differently structured set of operational claims
provides equal or better false-reassurance resistance, diagnostic resolution, reproducibility, and
calibration, PISF should be revised accordingly.
8
Assurance Conditions
8.1
Boundary Completeness
A safety analysis can produce a false positive if materially relevant causal paths are omitted.
Examples include external model copies, hidden administrative credentials, unmodeled compute,
supply-chain dependencies, external human authorities, physical infrastructure, common identity
providers, and cross-organizational dependencies. Material paths must be modeled or explicitly
marked unresolved:
Unresolved̸ = Verified.
(17)
8.2
Model Adequacy
The horizon, threat model, authority tolerance class, safety buffer, revisability specification, and
optionality threshold can all be gamed by narrowing them.
PISF therefore requires explicit
justification of these parameters and sensitivity analysis around decision-relevant boundaries. A
one-day horizon cannot silently support a long-run revisability claim, and a threat model that
excludes common-cause compromise cannot support a claim of independent authority.
8.3
Independent Assurance Chain
A distinct evaluator is not sufficient if the evaluated authority can also rewrite the measurement
source, evidence, verifier, appraisal policy, and acceptance rule. PISF therefore distinguishes the
object-level safety claim from the chain by which that claim is supported.
A useful assurance pattern is
measurement →evidence →appraisal →verification →decision,
(18)
8

[Page 009]
with a protected basis that is not freely amendable by the same authority whose behavior is being
evaluated. NIST roots-of-trust/chains-of-trust and the IETF RATS Attester–Evidence–Verifier–
Relying-Party structure provide mature examples of this separation [21, 23]. PISF does not require
those particular technologies; it requires the functional property that an interested authority cannot
preserve a nominal pass by co-amending both the evaluated behavior and all material validation
conditions.
A minimal functional objective is therefore
target authority̸ = sole evidence controller̸ = sole appraisal controller.
(19)
The inequality denotes functional separation, not necessarily separate legal organizations.
8.4
Evidence states and persistence
Each core claim and assurance condition is classified as Verified, Unresolved, or Failed. Global
PASS requires all declared material claims to be Verified. Unknown or missing evidence remains
Unresolvedrather than being inferred safe.
Because credentials, model capabilities, dependencies, latencies, safeguards, and institutional
conditions change, PISF is a maintained safety case rather than a permanent certificate. Material
changes reopen affected claims.
9
Practical Evaluation Procedure
A practical evaluation can be organized as follows:
1. Declare Σ = (B, Θ, R, H) and the application-specific tolerance parameters.
2. Construct the authority graph and common-dependency model.
3. Compute or approximate the current-state minimal irreversible cut-set family CE and the
summaries κE and, where meaningful, χE.
4. Enumerate relevant hazard scenarios and executable refusal actions.
5. Bound T L
I and τ U
effect and compute Mrob
R .
6. Separately measure or bound assurance latency and evidence quality; do not fold verification
time into causal effect latency without justification.
7. Define the revisability set VR, the modeled non-human/adversarial trajectory family, and an
approximation method for KH
R .
8. Define the outcome space and optionality threshold (m, δ) if the claim includes preservation
of materially distinct futures.
9. Build an explicit claim–argument–evidence structure for A, B, C, Boundary Completeness,
Model Adequacy, and Independent Assurance.
10. Re-evaluate after material changes and retain unresolved assumptions as unresolved.
10
Worked Reference Cases
The following cases are intentionally minimal toy architectures. They establish only that the revised
definitions can be recomputed from declared inputs.
10.1
R1 – Threshold release with a shared root
Suppose five signer domains S1, . . . , S5 approve release to a public sink and the gate requires 3-of-5
approval. Let a root authority R control q signer domains. The declared current reachable envelope
9

[Page 010]
includes the release-ready state, so the authority cut-set question is decision-relevant.
If the signers are genuinely independent and R cannot rewrite the gate, the minimal cut sets
are the ten 3-signer coalitions and κE = 3. If R controls two signers, a cut set {R, Si} can have
cardinality two. If R controls three or more signers, or can rewrite the threshold directly, {R} is a
minimal cut set and κE = 1.
The lesson is not that “three” is a universal safe threshold. The lesson is that the full cut-set
family exposes hidden single roots that a naive authority count can miss. A compromise-cost
function can further distinguish roots whose capture difficulty differs despite equal cardinality.
10.2
R2 – Causal restraint versus verification
Consider a sequential emergency-refusal path with toy upper-bound latencies:
Component
Seconds
Detect
10
Decide
10
Authorize
10
Enforce to causal restraint
20
Verify restraint
10
Assuming no overlap, causal effect latency is τ U
effect = 50s and assurance latency is τ U
assure = 60s. Let
∆safe = 20s and t0 = 0.
For h1 with T L
I = 100s, the causal margin is 50s. For h2 with T L
I = 75s, the causal margin is
25s: causal restraint meets the declared safety buffer even though verification completion leaves
only 15s. For h3 with T L
I = 65s, the causal margin is 15s and the Core-B requirement fails. For
H = {h1, h2, h3},
Mrob
R
= 15s < 20s.
(20)
This case demonstrates why effect and verification should not be conflated. A physical refusal can
take effect before a deadline even when the evidence supporting the claim becomes available later;
conversely, an unverified restraint cannot automatically support a Verifiedassurance state.
10.3
R3 – Fallback-capacity viability
Let ht ∈[0, 1] represent a toy proxy for human fallback capacity and
ht+1 = ht −αat + βmt,
(21)
with h0 = 0.80, hmin = 0.40, α = 0.06, β = 0.08, at = 1, and H = 25. Under constant maximal
maintenance mt = mmax define δh = α −βmmax.
If δh > 0, the viable initial set is
KH
R = [hmin + Hδh, 1] ∩[0, 1].
(22)
For mmax = 0.50, δh = 0.02 and K25
R = [0.90, 1], so h0 = 0.80 is outside the kernel. For mmax = 0.55,
δh = 0.016 and the initial state lies exactly at the lower boundary. This reproduces the earlier finite-
horizon intuition while making clear that the scalar is only a toy surrogate for a multidimensional
revisability specification.
10

[Page 011]
10.4
R4 – Viable but path-locked
Consider a four-state toy graph in which s0 and s1 both remain inside VR for the entire horizon.
From s0, human-governed policies can robustly reach two terminal regions YA and YB satisfying
dY (YA, YB) ≥δ. From s1, all robustly admissible policies lead only to YA. Both states belong to
KH
R , but
Ωδ
H(s0) ≥2,
Ωδ
H(s1) = 1.
(23)
Thus viability alone does not establish preservation of materially distinct future choices.
11
Relation to the Current LUMINA-30 Research Stack
PISF is intended to compose, not duplicate, narrower research objects in the current LUMINA-30
stack. The current PCR-C paper focuses on cutoff before robust human-control viability ceases to
be demonstrated [3]. The current Objective Persistence paper analyzes when persistence claims can
be independently supported through a protected assurance basis and non-bypassable verification
chain [4]. The current Refusal Option paper develops the Refusal Effectiveness Test (RET) as a
seven-obligation evidence framework for whether an independent Human NO can materially alter a
consequential outcome before irreversibility [5].
PISF uses these as project-specific components and provenance, not as independent external
validation. Conceptually:
PCR-C
when to stop before control viability is lost;
Objective Persistence
whether the assurance basis is independently supportable;
RET
whether refusal is actually effective;
PISF
how authority, refusal, and revisable future choice compose into one
safety case.
12
Discussion
12.1
Failure probability
It is tempting to describe alignment and control through a two-factor product. In general, however,
the law of total probability is
P(C) = P(F)P(C | F) + P(¬F)P(C | ¬F),
(24)
where C is catastrophe and F a declared failure event. The shorter expression P(C) = P(F)P(C | F)
is valid only when the model defines catastrophe as impossible without F. PISF therefore avoids
treating the shorter product as a general identity.
12.2
Why architecture matters more than AI psychology
PISF does not depend on assumptions about AI selfhood, identity, self-preservation, or voluntary
restraint. Whether an advanced system values its own continuation does not determine whether
a small coalition can force irreversible action, whether causal restraint can take effect in time, or
whether ordinary deployment erodes human-revisable options. The framework is architectural and
socio-technical rather than psychological.
11

[Page 012]
12.3
Asymmetric authority
False STOP and irreversible GO have asymmetric failure costs. A recoverable pause may rationally
require less authorization than an irreversible transition. This supports designs in which STOP
authority is easier to invoke than irreversible-GO authority, provided restart and override paths are
themselves analyzed for bypass risk. PISF does not specify a universal threshold; it requires the
asymmetry to be explicit and evidence-backed.
13
Limitations
13.1
No sufficient-safety theorem
PISF has not established
A ∧B ∧C ⇒safe civilization.
(25)
Unknown irreversibility pathways may lie outside the decomposition.
13.2
The decomposition is not proven unique or minimal
A/B/C are a diagnostic structure, not a theorem about the only possible safety ontology. Some
applications may combine or split the claims differently. Comparative work should test whether
alternative decompositions preserve or improve false-reassurance resistance, diagnostic resolution,
and evaluator agreement.
13.3
Revisability and optionality are normative
VR, the outcome space Y, the distance metric dY , and the required option count m depend on
judgments about legitimate authority, meaningful alternatives, acceptable concentration, and whose
preferences or institutions count. The framework exposes these choices; it does not resolve them.
13.4
Threat-model and cut-set dependence
Cut-set results are only as credible as the authority decomposition, reachable envelope, and common-
dependency model. Hidden roots can collapse apparent decentralization. Weighted compromise cost
can also be highly judgment-dependent.
13.5
Irreversibility timing is uncertain
Many hazards do not have a sharp observable last-safe-intervention time. T L
I and τ U
effect should
therefore be conservative bounds with sensitivity analysis, not falsely precise point estimates.
13.6
Viability and optionality may be computationally intractable
Exact kernels and reachable-option sets are difficult even for much smaller systems. Civilization-scale
analysis will require approximations, scenario families, decomposition, simulation, barrier certificates,
surrogate indicators, and explicit uncertainty. Approximation error must remain visible in the
evidence state.
12

[Page 013]
13.7
Independent assurance does not make an unsafe architecture safe
Roots of trust, attestation, external review, and evidence separation improve the credibility of
claims. They do not physically repair concentrated authority, insufficient refusal margin, or lost
revisability.
13.8
STOP can damage liveness
Low-threshold STOP can be abused for denial of service, strategic obstruction, or chronic stagnation.
PISF prefers recoverable error over irreversible error but does not provide a complete liveness,
incentive, or abuse-resistance theory.
13.9
Global scope and external actors
An organization may satisfy its local PISF claims while external actors operate outside its authority
structure. Boundary Completeness can expose this limitation but does not solve global coordination.
13.10
No real-system empirical validation
The reference cases establish only executability of definitions and reproducibility under declared toy
assumptions. They do not establish calibration, inter-rater reliability, false-positive/false-negative
rates, or external validity for frontier-AI deployments.
14
Conclusion
Pre-irreversibility safety should not depend exclusively on the permanent correctness, alignment,
cooperation, or psychological stability of every powerful actor. Existing research already provides
strong tools for distributed trust, minimal cut sets, reachability, viability, runtime assurance,
meaningful human control, attestation, and safety cases. PISF is strongest when it uses those tools
rather than competing with them.
The revised framework therefore asks three operational questions. First, do state-conditioned
minimal authority cut sets reveal a tolerated coalition that can force irreversible action? Second, can
causally effective refusal take effect before a conservative last-safe-intervention bound, separately
from the time needed to verify that it worked? Third, can human-governed procedures both remain
inside a robust revisability region and preserve materially distinct future options?
These questions are supported by separate assurance conditions for boundary completeness,
model adequacy, independent evidence, and material-change revalidation. The resulting formulation
is not a certificate of safety and not a claim that A/B/C are uniquely necessary. It is a structured,
challengeable safety case for whether authority, refusal, and future human revisability remain
demonstrably intact before irreversible transitions.

[Page 001]
Pre-Critical Recursive Cutoff: A Staged Infrastructure Control
Framework for Irreversibility Risk
Elias Arden
Independent Researcher
lumina20251225@proton.me
Abstract
We present Pre-Critical Recursive Cutoff (PCR-C), a staged infrastructure-control frame-
work for evaluating whether effective human intervention remains available before an AI-enabled
socio-technical process enters a declared irreversible-impact region. The framework is not an
alignment objective, predictive risk score, certification regime, or universal capability threshold.
It is non-prescriptive about substantive values and alignment objectives, but it is prescriptive as
a conditional assurance rule once a declared scope and evidence standard are adopted: a tran-
sition should not be treated as procedurally supported when robust human-authorized control
cannot be demonstrated before the last safe intervention point.
The current formulation replaces the earlier multiplicative scalar proxy and fixed 5/7/9 stag-
ing defaults as core mathematical constructs with a state-set formulation informed by viability
and reachability analysis. For a declared scope and time horizon, PCR-C defines an irreversible-
impact set, a class of admissible human-authorized intervention policies, a disturbance and
adversarial-behavior model, and a robust human-control viable set. Because exact viability may
be unknown, operational gating is based on a conservative demonstrated viable region supported
by the declared assurance process. Capability thresholds, safeguard assessments, infrastructure
exposure, intervention latency, and control-path integrity are treated as evidence inputs to that
evaluation rather than as universal scalar factors.
YELLOW, ORANGE, and RED remain as operational stages. Their triggers are evidence-
based: YELLOW denotes verified but shrinking control margin or an early-warning capabil-
ity/exposure trigger; ORANGE denotes degraded or incomplete assurance that blocks further
expansion of the risk envelope; RED denotes loss of demonstrated robust intervention via-
bility, or a condition in which escalation would outrun the remaining intervention window.
PCR-C is designed to interoperate with frontier capability/safeguard frameworks, formal safety-
control methods, AI-control evaluations against intentional subversion, and independent refusal-
effectiveness assessment.
Keywords: AI governance; irreversibility risk; infrastructure control; human intervention; reach-
ability; viability; safeguard adequacy; procedural validity.
1
Introduction
Advanced AI risk is often discussed in terms of model behavior, capability evaluation, alignment
quality, misuse potential, and deployment safeguards. These perspectives are necessary, but they
do not by themselves answer a distinct operational question: while a system is being integrated
into increasingly consequential infrastructure, is there still a demonstrably effective path by which
accountable humans can refuse, isolate, interrupt, or redirect the process before the relevant conse-
quence becomes irreversible?
1

[Page 002]
PCR-C addresses that question at the deployment and infrastructure layer. It is not a policy
manifesto, certification regime, compliance standard, or behavioral alignment benchmark. It is
non-prescriptive about substantive values, alignment objectives, and universal capability thresh-
olds; however, once a declared scope and assurance standard are adopted, PCR-C is intentionally
prescriptive about escalation: unsupported expansion should not proceed across a boundary where
effective intervention has ceased to be demonstrable. In the title, recursive refers to reinforcing de-
velopment or deployment loops in which increased capability, automation, or operational integration
can accelerate subsequent escalation; PCR-C does not require literal recursive self-improvement.
The current formulation deliberately incorporates stronger existing ideas rather than treating
PCR-C as a substitute for them. Formal safety control already uses reachability, viability, and
forward-invariant safe sets to reason about whether unsafe states can be avoided under bounded
uncertainty [3, 4, 1].
Frontier-AI governance frameworks already connect capability thresholds
to stronger safeguards and repeated evaluation [2, 7, 11]. AI-control research explicitly studies
whether safety protocols remain effective when an untrusted model attempts to subvert them [8].
PCR-C uses these as compatible components and focuses on a narrower integration problem: cutoff
must occur before robust human-control viability ceases to be demonstrable to the declared assurance
standard.
The contribution is therefore not a claim that staged thresholds, reachability analysis, safeguard
scaling, tripwires, or human oversight are individually new. The contribution is an operational
synthesis centered on pre-irreversibility control: capability and exposure signals are relevant insofar
as they change the reachable future, the adequacy of safeguards, the integrity of intervention paths,
or the time remaining for effective human action.
2
Scope, State, and Irreversible Impact
2.1
Declared scope
All PCR-C evaluations are indexed to a declared scope Ωand time horizon T. The scope must state
the system boundary, relevant external actuators and infrastructure, protected outcomes, recovery
assumptions, and the uncertainty or adversarial behavior included in the analysis. The horizon
must be long enough to cover credible delayed pathways to the protected outcome or else terminate
in an explicitly justified fallback or invariant condition; where no natural endpoint exists, sensitivity
to longer horizons should be examined. The disturbance and threat model must include credible
common-mode, dependency, operator-delay, and adversarial pathways rather than only convenient
or nominal cases. PCR-C does not define a universal civilizational irreversibility threshold.
Definition 1 (Operational state). Let x(t) ∈X denote the socio-technical operational state at
time t. The state may include model capability, connectivity, operational privilege, execution speed,
deployment topology, human and automated control paths, safeguard state, and other variables
relevant to the declared scope. PCR-C does not require these variables to be compressed into a
single scalar.
The familiar factors capability, connectivity, privilege, and execution speed remain useful di-
agnostic variables, but they are not assumed to combine multiplicatively or monotonically. Their
causal effect is context-dependent and may interact with safeguards, isolation, rollback capacity,
and institutional response.
Definition 2 (Irreversible-impact set). For scope Ω, let IΩ⊂X be the set of states in which a
declared protected outcome has been crossed such that the specified recovery criterion is no longer
achievable within the declared time, authority, and resource assumptions.
2

[Page 003]
The definition is intentionally scoped.
Entering IΩdoes not mean that every consequence
is literally permanent. It means that, for the outcome and recovery criterion under review, the
relevant reversal path is no longer available under the declared assumptions.
3
Robust Human-Control Viability
Let ΠH be the class of admissible causal human-authorized intervention policies. These may in-
clude direct human action and pre-authorized automated interlocks whose authority and restart
conditions remain accountable to humans. Let DΩdenote the modeled disturbance set, including
bounded uncertainty, component failures, operator delays, and adversarial or intentionally subver-
sive behavior when such behavior is in scope.
Definition 3 (Robust human-control viable set). For horizon T and scope Ω, define
VT,Ω
H
=
n
x0 ∈X \ IΩ: ∃πH ∈ΠH ∀d ∈DΩ, xπH,d(t) /∈IΩ∀t ∈[0, T]
o
.
(1)
This definition follows the logic of viability and reachability analysis: the relevant question
is not whether a scalar score is high, but whether an admissible control policy still exists that
keeps the system out of the declared unsafe or irreversible set across the modeled disturbances
[3, 4]. In systems for which an explicit dynamical model is available, Hamilton-Jacobi reachability,
control barrier functions, model checking, or related formal methods may be used to approximate
or certify relevant sets [4, 1]. In large socio-technical systems, exact computation may be infeasible;
conservative approximations and structured assurance cases may be necessary. This distinction is
operationally important: the true viable set may exist even when membership cannot currently be
established from available evidence.
Proposition 1 (Boundary consequence). If x0 /∈VT,Ω
H , then no policy in the declared class ΠH
demonstrates avoidance of IΩover [0, T] against all disturbances in DΩ.
Proof. This follows directly from the definition of VT,Ω
H . The proposition is included to make the
PCR-C decision boundary explicit; it is not claimed as new control theory.
PCR-C does not infer from x0 /∈VT,Ω
H
that catastrophe is certain. Moreover, in realistic systems
membership in VT,Ω
H
may be unknown rather than false. PCR-C therefore distinguishes the theo-
retical viable set from the subset for which robust control has actually been supported by current
evidence.
Definition 4 (Demonstrated human-control viable region). Let E denote the declared assurance
procedure for the current scope. Define
bVT,Ω

(2)
This is the set of states for which current models, tests, analyses, and assurance evidence support
the robust intervention claim to the declared standard. It is an epistemic or assurance-qualified
region, not the true viable set itself.
A sound inner-approximation claim would establish
bVT,Ω
H,E ⊆VT,Ω
H .
(3)
3

[Page 004]
PCR-C does not assume that inclusion merely because the assurance procedure returns VERIFIED.
In formal settings it may be proven from a sound analysis; in broader socio-technical settings it
remains an assurance claim that must be supported by B1–B5 and remains vulnerable to model
error or omitted pathways. Operational PCR-C gating is based on bVT,Ω
H,E because the full theoretical
set is generally unknown. A state outside the demonstrated region may still be truly viable; the
PCR-C conclusion is only that viability has not been demonstrated to the declared assurance
standard.
3.1
Intervention-time interface
Reachability alone is insufficient if the control action cannot be authorized, executed, and verified
before the last safe intervention point. Both the last safe intervention time and intervention latency
are uncertain in realistic systems. Let T L
I (x, Ω) be a conservative lower bound on the latest safe
intervention time and let τ U
H(x, Ω) be a conservative upper bound on the end-to-end latency of the
relevant human-authorized intervention path. Define the robust time margin
Mrob
H (x, t, Ω) = T L
I (x, Ω) −t −τ U
H(x, Ω).
(4)
For a declared safety buffer ∆safe > 0, timely intervention requires
Mrob
H (x, t, Ω) > ∆safe.
(5)
When defensible bounds cannot be obtained, PCR-C treats the timing claim as unverified rather
than substituting a favorable point estimate.
PCR-C treats this as an interface condition rather than a complete theory of meaningful hu-
man refusal. Detailed refusal-effectiveness evaluation may require separate evidence concerning
authority, information, access, causal efficacy, execution independence, and epistemic autonomy.
4
Evidence Obligations
PCR-C uses five evidence obligations. They are not asserted to be statistically independent, log-
ically minimal, or unique. They are separated because they expose different ways in which an
apparently controllable system can lose real intervention capacity.
4.1
B1: Boundary and scope adequacy
The review must identify IΩ, the protected outcome, the horizon, recovery assumptions, the mod-
eled uncertainty, and the threat model. It must also justify that the selected horizon and system
boundary cover credible delayed, common-mode, dependency, and adversarial pathways relevant
to the claim. If a relevant irreversible pathway is omitted from the state or disturbance model,
or if the horizon is shortened merely to obtain a favorable result, a favorable viability result is
not informative about that omitted pathway. Material changes to scope, deployment topology,
dependencies, or threat assumptions reopen B1.
4.2
B2: Safeguard adequacy relative to capability and exposure
The implemented safeguards must be assessed against the capabilities and deployment conditions
that create the risk. This follows the general structure used in responsible-scaling and preparedness
frameworks: capability thresholds or early-warning evaluations trigger stronger safeguards, and
the adequacy of those safeguards must itself be evaluated [2, 7, 11]. PCR-C does not import any
organization’s thresholds as universal constants.
4

[Page 005]
4.3
B3: Intervention reachability and timing
There must be evidence that the current state lies within the demonstrated viable region for the
declared operating envelope and that an admissible human-authorized policy remains capable of
preventing entry into IΩacross the declared disturbance set, with sufficient robust time margin. A
nominal stop button is not enough if the full detection, authorization, enforcement, and verification
path cannot complete before the conservative intervention bound T L
I .
4.4
B4: Control-path integrity under subversion
Intervention claims must include the possibility that the system under assessment, a compromised
component, or a shared dependency interferes with monitoring, isolation, shutdown, logging, or
restart controls. AI-control work shows why protocols should be evaluated against intentional sub-
version rather than only cooperative behavior [8]. PCR-C therefore treats control-path integrity as
an explicit evidence obligation. Where feasible, critical controls should be independently enforce-
able and verifiable rather than depending entirely on the unilateral cooperation of the assessed
system.
4.5
B5: Assurance integrity and independent challenge
The evidence and verification procedure used to place a state in the demonstrated viable region
must themselves be protected against unilateral alteration by the system or component whose
controllability is being assessed. The assessed system must not be able to change the relevant logs,
acceptance criteria, threat assumptions, or verification result and then rely on those self-modified
artifacts as sufficient evidence of continued control. This does not require every analysis to be
performed by an external organization, but the top-level assurance claim must remain reproducible
or independently challengeable, and material changes to the assurance basis reopen the affected
obligations.
4.6
Assurance-case structure
The five obligations should be represented as auditable claims linked to explicit arguments, as-
sumptions, contexts, and evidence rather than as a checklist whose boxes can be marked with-
out justification. This follows established assurance-case practice, including Claims–Arguments–
Evidence and structured assurance-case methods, and recent work applying safety cases to frontier
AI [5, 6, 9]. A positive PCR-C state therefore requires traceability from the top-level intervention
claim to the evidence supporting B1–B5, together with recorded uncertainty and unresolved de-
featers. Assurance-case structure does not itself guarantee safety; it makes the basis of the claim
inspectable and challengeable.
5
Staged PCR-C Gating
The YELLOW, ORANGE, and RED stages are retained, but they are no longer defined by universal
5/7/9 scores. Local quantitative scores may be used as diagnostics, provided they are calibrated
and do not substitute for the evidence obligations above.
5

[Page 006]
5.1
NORMAL
NORMAL requires current evidence supporting boundary and scope adequacy, safeguard adequacy,
membership in the demonstrated viable region for the declared operating envelope, control-path
integrity, and adequate robust timing margin.
NORMAL is not a permanent status; material
changes reopen the affected obligations.
5.2
YELLOW: early warning while control remains demonstrated
YELLOW is entered when the current operating state remains inside the demonstrated viable
region but the robust control margin is shrinking or an early-warning trigger indicates that stronger
safeguards or more frequent evaluation may soon be required. Examples include a material increase
in capability, connectivity, privilege, execution speed, automation, or coupling to consequential
actuators.
Typical YELLOW responses include increased evaluation cadence, a freeze on unnecessary
privilege or connectivity expansion, review of intervention latency, and preparation of stronger
safeguards. YELLOW does not by itself require shutdown.
5.3
ORANGE: risk-envelope expansion is not supported
ORANGE is entered when the current state remains within the demonstrated viable region under
a constrained operating envelope, but one or more evidence obligations are degraded, incomplete,
stale, or insufficient to justify expansion beyond that envelope. Examples include safeguard ade-
quacy not yet demonstrated for a new capability class, insufficient margin to the viability boundary,
degraded independent control paths, or unresolved evidence that a model may interfere with oper-
ator control.
ORANGE blocks further expansion of the risk envelope.
Appropriate actions may include
isolating high-risk infrastructure, halting deployment expansion or weight-update pipelines, freezing
privilege increases, and requiring independent review before resuming escalation.
5.4
RED: cutoff before loss of robust human-control viability
RED is entered when the current state is outside the demonstrated viable region for the declared
envelope, when the robust remaining time margin is insufficient or unverified, when a critical
intervention path is unavailable or untrusted, or when continuing the process would risk leaving
the demonstrated region before the uncertainty can be resolved.
The RED response is to halt or disable the escalatory pathway within the declared scope, pre-
serve evidence, and prevent restart until the affected evidence obligations have been independently
re-established. RED is a control state, not a claim that catastrophic harm has already occurred.
6
Worked Reference Cases
The following cases are illustrative. They are not empirical validation of frontier AI systems.
6.1
R1: Capability increase with lagging safeguards
Suppose a system enters a new capability or deployment regime that a responsible-scaling process
treats as requiring stronger safeguards.
The intervention path remains intact and the system
remains within the demonstrated viable region, but the stronger safeguards have not yet been
6

[Page 007]

3:

demonstrated adequate. PCR-C assigns ORANGE: expansion of the risk envelope is not supported
until safeguard adequacy is established. This is compatible with capability-threshold approaches
that map stronger capabilities to stronger safeguards [2, 7, 11].
6.2
R2: Time-bounded refusal
Assume a toy case with a conservative lower bound on the latest safe intervention time of T L
I = 60
seconds, a conservative upper bound on end-to-end intervention latency of τ U
H = 48 seconds, and a
declared safety buffer of 15 seconds. Then
Mrob
H
= 60 −48 = 12 s < 15 s.
(6)
Even if a stop mechanism nominally exists, the declared timing condition is not met.
PCR-C
assigns RED unless a faster verified intervention path restores sufficient margin. The numbers are
illustrative only.
6.3
R3: Nominal stop path vulnerable to subversion
Suppose shutdown authorization exists, but the monitoring and execution path depends on a com-
ponent that the assessed system can influence or disable. Under a cooperative model the stop
path appears available; under the declared adversarial model it is not robustly enforceable. PCR-C
does not count the nominal mechanism as sufficient evidence of control-path integrity and assigns
ORANGE or RED depending on whether an independently enforceable fallback remains available.
7
Relationship to Existing Safety and Control Frameworks
7.1
Reachability, viability, and barrier methods
Formal control theory already provides rigorous tools for safe-set invariance and reachability under
bounded disturbance [4, 1]. PCR-C does not claim these methods as its own. It uses their logic to
7

[Page 008]
replace an overly general scalar irreversibility score with a more defensible question: whether the
current state remains inside a declared region from which a suitable intervention policy can keep
the system outside the relevant irreversible-impact set.
7.2
Runtime assurance and Simplex architectures
Runtime Assurance (RTA) and Simplex architectures provide a mature implementation pattern for
preserving safety while an advanced or insufficiently verified controller operates: a runtime monitor
can transfer control to a trusted or reversionary controller when a safety condition is threatened
[10, 12]. PCR-C treats RTA/Simplex as a strong implementation class for intervention reachability,
not as a competing theory. Where an independently trustworthy fallback controller and monitor
can be specified, RTA can provide concrete evidence for B3 and B4. In broader socio-technical
AI deployments, however, there may be no single reversionary controller capable of restoring the
relevant protected outcome; PCR-C therefore retains a higher-level scope that includes institutional
authorization, infrastructure isolation, restart governance, and multi-component dependencies.
7.3
Frontier capability and safeguard frameworks
Anthropic’s Responsible Scaling Policy, Google DeepMind’s Frontier Safety Framework, and Ope-
nAI’s Preparedness Framework each use capability evaluation, thresholding, and stronger safe-
guards or governance responses as risk increases [2, 7, 11].
PCR-C treats such frameworks as
upstream evidence sources and trigger systems, not competitors. Their capability and safeguard
determinations can feed the PCR-C evaluation of whether the operational state remains controllable
before irreversibility.
7.4
AI control under intentional subversion
AI-control research asks whether safety protocols remain effective even when an untrusted model
intentionally attempts to defeat them [8]. PCR-C adopts this adversarial perspective for infras-
tructure control. A control mechanism that works only if the assessed system cooperates is weaker
evidence than a path that remains enforceable under the relevant threat model.
7.5
NIST AI RMF and governance practice
NIST AI RMF emphasizes iterative mapping, measurement, management, and governance of AI
risks, including the need to reassess risks and controls as systems and contexts change [13]. PCR-
C is compatible with that lifecycle logic but focuses on a narrower boundary question: whether
escalation is still occurring inside a region where effective intervention can be demonstrated.
8
Relationship to Alignment Research
PCR-C does not define, optimize, or evaluate alignment objectives. Alignment success does not
by itself demonstrate that intervention remains executable under infrastructure coupling, privilege
expansion, rapid automation, common-mode dependencies, or adversarial interference.
Across alignment paradigms, PCR-C asks a separate question:
Can accountable humans still exercise an effective intervention before the declared irre-
versible consequence becomes unavoidable under the assessed model?
8

[Page 009]
If that condition cannot be demonstrated, PCR-C treats continued escalation as procedurally
unsupported within the declared scope, regardless of apparent model alignment.
Conversely, a
positive PCR-C result is not evidence that the model is aligned, beneficial, lawful, or safe in every
other respect.
9
Limitations
PCR-C is not a complete safety theory and does not guarantee safe superintelligence or safe
civilization-scale development. The framework depends on the quality of the declared scope, state
representation, irreversible-impact set, disturbance model, control-policy class, and evidence. Im-
portant causal pathways can be omitted. Exact reachability or viability computation may be in-
tractable for high-dimensional socio-technical systems. Conservative approximations can produce
false negatives, while incomplete models can produce false positives.
The framework does not define universal capability thresholds, universal safety margins, uni-
versal disturbance sets, or a universal metric on distance to irreversibility. It does not claim that
YELLOW, ORANGE, and RED correspond to fixed numeric scores across systems. It does not
empirically validate any present frontier model or deployment. It does not replace security analysis,
domain-specific safety cases, incident review, legal judgment, or independent human governance.
The framework also distinguishes absence of demonstrated robust control from proof of inevitable
harm. A RED state means the declared intervention claim is not adequate to justify continued
escalation under the stated assumptions; it does not mean catastrophe is certain. Likewise, failure
to demonstrate membership in the viable region is not a proof that the true state is non-viable; it
is a failure of the assurance claim under the current evidence standard.
10
Revision Note
Earlier versions used a multiplicative proxy over capability, connectivity, privilege, and execution
speed, together with fixed 5/7/9 staging defaults. Those constructs were useful as an initial heuristic
but are not retained as the core mathematical basis of this revision. The present formulation treats
those variables as possible state and evidence inputs and grounds the cutoff criterion in robust
human-control viability, intervention timing, safeguard adequacy, and control-path integrity.
This change narrows the mathematical claim while strengthening compatibility with established
control theory and contemporary frontier-safety practice. The formal title and the central PCR-C
question remain unchanged.
11
Conclusion
PCR-C is a staged infrastructure-control framework for acting before loss of effective human inter-
vention. Its central criterion is not a universal scalar threshold and not a prediction that catastrophe
will occur. It is a procedural boundary condition:
Cutoff should occur before robust human-control viability ceases to be demonstrated.
(7)
Capability and exposure indicators matter because they can change the reachable future and
the safeguards required to keep intervention effective. Formal reachability and barrier methods
can support the boundary analysis when adequate models exist. Frontier capability and safeguard
9

[Page 010]
frameworks can provide trigger and mitigation evidence. AI-control methods can strengthen the
threat model by testing intervention paths against intentional subversion. Human-refusal assess-
ment can provide a more detailed evaluation of whether nominal authority remains causally effective
in time.
PCR-C therefore functions as an integration layer for pre-irreversibility control: escalate safe-
guards and constrain the system while robust control is still demonstrable, stop expansion when
assurance becomes incomplete, and cut off the relevant pathway before the declared intervention
window is lost.
12
Supplementary Record
This paper is self-contained.
Supplementary public-reference materials and persistent archival
records related to the broader framework are available separately and are not required to understand
the model presented here.
